Chapter 3
Mathematical Modeling for the Calculation of Porous Structure Properties: Techniques and Applications

3.1 INTRODUCTION

Designing porous structures from the bottom up requires a comprehensive approach that integrates pore‐level design with the overall structural framework. Achieving this necessitates a methodology capable of linking microscale features to macroscale behavior. This chapter presents the foundational principles of material properties and microstructure, alongside the historical evolution of methods that ultimately led to the development of the homogenization technique. Recognized for its efficiency, homogenization serves as a versatile and cost‐effective strategy for designing advanced microstructures in diverse applications.

3.2 OVERVIEW OF COMPUTATIONAL ASSESSMENTS OF PHYSICAL PROPERTIES OF MATERIALS

The foundational tenets of atomic theory form the cornerstone of contemporary chemistry, offering insights into the intricate composition and inherent properties of matter. The evolution of atomic theory spans over two millennia, beginning with the philosophical contemplations of Aristotle and Democritus. Aristotle proposed that matter could be infinitely divisible without altering its fundamental nature, whereas Democritus introduced the notion of an indivisible entity, termed the “atom,” derived from the Greek word “atomos,” signifying indivisibility. This intellectual discourse persisted until the early 19th century when John Dalton, through rigorous experimentation, provided empirical validation for the existence of atoms. Dalton's atomic theory posited that matter is composed of indivisible particles known as atoms, which are neither created nor destroyed. He further asserted that all atoms of a particular element are identical in mass and properties and that compounds are formed by the combination of distinct types of atoms in fixed ratios. While Dalton's model was groundbreaking, it lacked the understanding of subatomic particles such as neutrons and electrons, which are yet to be discovered.

The advancement of atomic theory gained momentum in the late 19th and early 20th centuries, with pivotal contributions from physicists like J.J. Thomson and Ernest Rutherford. Thomson's cathode ray experiments led to the discovery of the electron, a negatively charged subatomic particle, thus challenging Dalton's concept of indivisible atoms. Thomson introduced the plum pudding model, which envisioned electrons embedded within a positively charged matrix. This model was later refuted by Rutherford's gold foil experiment, which uncovered that atoms consist of a dense, positively charged nucleus surrounded by a cloud of electrons. Rutherford's nuclear model demonstrated that the atom is predominantly an empty space, with nearly all of its mass concentrated in the nucleus.

The subsequent refinement of atomic theory was achieved with Niels Bohr's quantized shell model in 1913, which elucidated the stability of electron orbits around the nucleus. Bohr introduced the concept of quantized energy levels, whereby electrons could only occupy specific orbits without emitting energy. This model laid the groundwork for modern quantum mechanics, which offers a more comprehensive understanding of atomic structure and electron behavior.

The exploration of atomic theory extends to the standard model of particle physics, which delineates the fundamental particles and forces constituting matter. The standard model identifies four fundamental forces: the strong force, the weak force, the electromagnetic force, and gravity. The strong and weak forces operate within the atomic nucleus, governing subatomic interactions, while the electromagnetic force and gravity possess infinite ranges but vary significantly in strength. The strong force, the most potent, binds protons and neutrons within the nucleus, whereas the weak force is responsible for radioactive decay. The electromagnetic force, far stronger than gravity, governs interactions between charged particles, while gravity, despite being the weakest force, exerts an infinite‐range influence on all matter.

Understanding atomic structure requires grasping key concepts such as atomic number, mass number, isotopes, and atomic mass. The atomic number, representing the number of protons in an atom, defines the element and remains constant across all isotopes of that element. For instance, carbon always has six protons. In a neutral atom, the number of electrons equals the number of protons, balancing the charges. The mass number, however, is the sum of protons and neutrons in the nucleus and varies among isotopes of the same element. Carbon, for example, has three naturally occurring isotopes: carbon‐12, carbon‐13, and carbon‐14, with mass numbers of 12, 13, and 14, respectively. The average atomic mass of an element, as listed on the periodic table, reflects the weighted average of all its isotopes based on their natural abundance.

Calculating the average atomic mass involves multiplying the mass of each isotope by its relative abundance and summing these values. For instance, copper has two stable isotopes with mass numbers of 63 and 65. If copper‐63 has an abundance of 69.2% and an atomic mass of 62.93 atomic mass units (amu) and copper‐65 has an abundance of 30.8% with an atomic mass of 64.93 amu, the average atomic mass is calculated as: (62.93 amu * 0.692) + (64.93 amu * 0.308), resulting in an average atomic mass of approximately 63.55 amu. This value corresponds with the atomic mass listed on the periodic table, demonstrating how the contributions of different isotopes are integrated to represent an element's average atomic mass.

For scientists and engineers, the properties of materials at the atomic and subatomic levels are fundamentally tied to the arrangement and energetic states of atoms. For example, the mechanical strength of a material depends on its specific characteristics. In metallic materials, strength is primarily governed by the freedom of electrons, which defines the metallic bonding and structure.

In solid materials, these bulk properties are often conceptualized through the averaging of electronic configurations and energy levels, considering the spatial dimensions of atomic nuclei. This abstraction is rooted in the understanding that electron behavior functions as the cohesive force binding atoms into a solid structure. Consequently, a core objective of condensed matter theory is to accurately determine the electronic structure (ES) of solids.

ES calculations, grounded in ab initio (first‐principles) methodologies, allow for predictions of the structural and dynamic properties of solids. These calculations are based on fundamental quantum theory, requiring the atomic numbers of constituent atoms and basic structural information as input. Within the framework of density functional theory (DFT), the complex many‐body problem of electron motion is reduced to a single electron moving in an effective potential. This approach has become a standard in computational material science, enabling the analysis and prediction of material properties with remarkable accuracy.

While the formulation of quantum mechanical equations for ESs (which account for all known interactions between electrons and atomic nuclei in solids) is conceptually straightforward, solving these equations in their entirety remains a formidable challenge due to their intrinsic complexity. To address this, various approximations are employed. Despite their simplifications, these approximations often yield thorough solutions.

The computational demands of solving the ES problem are immense, necessitating substantial computational resources. Before the advent of advanced, high‐speed computing, practical ES calculations in solids were exceedingly rare. However, the rapid development and increased availability of computational power as well as numerical methods have revolutionized the field, giving rise to the discipline of computational material science.

Advances in computational power, alongside the development of innovative computational methods and algorithms, have dramatically improved the precision of ES calculations. These advancements enable highly accurate solutions to the one‐electron Kohn–Sham equation for solids, providing unparalleled details at the atomic and electronic scales. This capability not only allows for the simulation of experimental conditions but also facilitates the design of new molecules and materials, predicting their properties prior to synthesis. Computational simulations offer atomic‐scale insights that are often inaccessible through experimental methods, providing a complementary approach to empirical studies. Unlike semi‐empirical methods, which rely on adjustable parameters tailored to the properties of ground‐state structures and may lack transferability to non‐equilibrium configurations, ab initio calculations remain reliable even under non‐equilibrium conditions. This reliability renders them indispensable in multi‐scale modeling of materials, serving two critical functions: (i) addressing scenarios where electronic effects are pivotal and require first‐principles treatment and (ii) generating data for the development of interatomic potentials.

The influence of computational materials science extends beyond merely predicting and understanding material properties. It also enables the design of new materials with customized properties, accelerating discovery and minimizing the need for costly and time‐intensive experimental trials. By harnessing high‐performance computing and advanced algorithms, researchers can explore vast parameter spaces, identifying promising material candidates for a wide range of applications, from electronics to energy storage and beyond.

As theoretical approaches and computational technologies continue to advance, the role of computational methods in materials science is expanding. With an in‐depth understanding of ESs and enhanced computational capabilities, the potential for discovering novel materials and uncovering new physical phenomena is growing.

Polymeric and ceramic materials, like their metallic counterparts, exhibit unique properties rooted in their molecular and atomic structures. Polymers, composed of long, repeating chains of molecules, are characterized by their versatility, lightweight nature, and wide range of mechanical behaviors, which can be tailored through chemical composition and processing techniques. Ceramics, on the other hand, are typically inorganic, non‐metallic materials that are prized for their hardness, thermal stability, and resistance to chemical degradation. Unlike metals, which exhibit ductility and malleability, ceramics are generally brittle, though advancements in ceramic engineering have led to the development of toughened ceramics with enhanced mechanical performance. Both polymeric and ceramic materials play critical roles in modern engineering applications, where their distinct properties are leveraged for specific functional requirements.

The advent of composite materials, which combine two or more distinct phases (typically a matrix and a reinforcement), has revolutionized engineering materials, offering a means to achieve superior properties unattainable by any single material alone. Composites are categorized based on the nature of their constituent materials and the arrangement of the reinforcing phase, leading to classifications such as fiber‐reinforced, particle‐reinforced, and structural composites. The design and optimization of composite structures demand developing numerical methods that deal with the vast range of possible material combinations and configurations, each yielding distinct mechanical properties. These methods are essential for predicting the behavior of composites under various loading conditions, enabling engineers to tailor material properties to specific applications with high precision.

As the complexity of composite structures increases, so too does the necessity for advanced numerical techniques to accurately calculate their mechanical properties. The semi‐infinite combinations of composite materials, coupled with the variability in reinforcement distribution, necessitate a departure from purely empirical approaches. Instead, computational methods have become indispensable for deriving the mechanical properties of composites from the experimentally measured properties of their base materials. This approach allows for the efficient exploration of the material design space, enabling the prediction and optimization of composite performance in a virtual environment before physical prototypes are fabricated.

3.3 MECHANICAL PROPERTIES OF MICROSTRUCTURE

The development of the design and numerical analysis of composite materials in recent years has been remarkable, leading to the emergence of materials with a variety of new mechanical and chemical properties. These advancements have catalyzed the consideration of composite materials for practical applications in various industrial sectors, particularly as structural members.

Despite the rapid material advancements, progress in understanding the mechanics of these new composites has lagged significantly. This discrepancy is primarily due to an insufficient grasp of the mechanical properties essential for developing structural mechanics tailored to composite materials. For composites to be effectively utilized in structures and buildings, it is imperative to advance not only the fundamental mechanics of composite materials but also their structural mechanics.

Currently, the research focus is heavily skewed toward the synthesis of composite materials and the exploration of basic fracture mechanics. This narrow focus has resulted in a fragile foundational understanding, which is inadequate for the application of composites as structural materials. The disproportionate emphasis on the microscopic aspects of composite materials often leads to the neglect of macroscopic perspectives. A holistic understanding that integrates both scales is crucial for the effective processing and application of composites. The processing of composite materials can only be truly feasible when there is a profound interconnection between macroscopic and microscopic mechanics.

Historically, research has dedicated substantial effort to deriving structural equations for the macroscopic mechanics of composite materials, equations that ostensibly reflect their microscopic structures. However, this approach has often overlooked the reciprocal influence, where macroscopic information could inform and optimize microscopic structures to attain better design. Consequently, this one‐sided focus has stymied the development of a better mechanics framework for processing composite materials. However, the challenge of computationally integrating stress and strain considerations across scales has delayed the evolution of processing mechanics and structural mechanics.

Numerical methods for evaluating composite materials are of immense importance as they not only enable the prediction of composite behavior based on the properties of their constituent materials but also assist in designing new composites with improved performance. Moreover, advancements in designing porous structures for additive manufacturing rely on accurately predicting the behavior of the microstructure—specifically, the pores and their interactions with the overall macrostructure, i.e., the topology of the porous structure.

As discussed in the following chapter, the asymptotic homogenization method, originally developed for evaluating composite materials, is one of the most widely used techniques today. Therefore, we will explore key methods for evaluating composite materials, starting with mixing rules and their limitations and culminating in advanced approaches that integrate varying fields, such as stress and strain analysis at the microscale.

3.3.1 Mixing Rule

Let us discuss the classical configuration of composite material mechanics which involves alternating materials of A and B in phasis, as depicted in Figure 3.1.

Let us derive the formula for such a model. Consider plates A and B with thicknesses ta and tb, respectively, arranged alternately. When stress is applied perpendicularly to the plates, the total strain can be considered as the sum of the strains in each individual plate.

(3.1)

In the linear elastic region, the above equation can be rewritten in stress form as

(3.2)

So, the equivalent elastic modulus of the composite material can be evaluated as

(3.3)

Such that

(3.4)

The parameters αa and αb represent the volume fractions of materials a and b, respectively, assuming that the areas of the plates are identical.

The classification of stress–strain relationships varies depending on the material configuration. As illustrated in Figure 3.2(a), when the displacement field is continuous across the material phases, the strain fields for both materials, A and B, within the composite are identical. Consequently, the overall stress is the combined contribution of the stresses from materials A and B. This scenario is referred to as the iso‐strain.

In contrast, the configuration depicted in Figure 3.2(b) exhibits uniform stress across materials A and B. In this case, the displacement and strain fields represent the combined contributions of materials A and B. This scenario is referred to as the iso‐stress.

The rule of mixtures provides a framework to estimate the effective properties of a composite based on the properties and volume fractions of its constituents. When considering the elastic modulus of a composite, two primary conditions—iso‐strain and iso‐stress—are used to establish the upper and lower bounds, respectively.

The iso‐strain condition represents an ideal case where there are perfect load transfer and intimate bonding between the phases. The effective modulus of the composite in this case is a simple weighted average of the moduli of the individual components, proportional to their respective volume fractions. This approach generally predicts the upper bound of the composite's modulus, reflecting the maximum stiffness achievable when the reinforcing phase is aligned with the direction of the applied stress.

Conversely, the iso‐stress condition assumes that the stress is uniformly distributed across the matrix and reinforcement phases. This scenario might occur when there is imperfect bonding or when the load is applied transversely to the reinforcement. Here, the effective modulus is calculated using a harmonic mean, where the volume fractions are inversely related to the moduli of the components. This method typically predicts the lower bound of the composite's modulus, representing the minimum stiffness, which might occur under suboptimal conditions, such as poor load transfer or misalignment of the reinforcement.

For an elastic two‐phase composite, the stiffness bounds provided by the Voigt and Reuss models serve as the main calculated bounds on the Young's modulus based on the volume fractions of the constituent phases. These bounds are applicable regardless of the geometric configuration of the phases within the composite. The Voigt model (upper bound in Figure 3.3), representing the upper bound, assumes a scenario where the strain is uniformly distributed across both phases. The Young's modulus for the Voigt composite is given by

(3.5)

Conversely, the Reuss model (lower bound in Figure 3.3), representing the lower bound, assumes that the stress is uniformly distributed across the phases. The Young's modulus for the Reuss composite is given by

(3.6)

where the reciprocal relationship reflects the series configuration of the phases, again neglecting Poisson's effects.

The representation of the Voigt–Reuss bounds presents certain limitations, particularly in their sensitivity to loading conditions. This variability introduces significant discrepancies in the calculation of composite material properties, especially within the design space—the region delineated between the upper and lower bounds as depicted in Figure 3.3. The inherent uncertainties in this range necessitate the application of substantial safety factors, which are especially critical in high‐risk domains such as aerospace engineering. The imposition of these safety factors often requires an increase in both the volume of the filler material and the overall size of the structural component. This, in turn, leads to a marked increase in weight, which severely compromises performance and can undermine the primary advantage of using composite materials in the first place—namely, their potential for lightweight, high‐strength applications. Consequently, while the Voigt–Reuss bounds offer a foundational framework for understanding the composite behavior, their limitations underscore the need for more precise methods.

To address these challenges, extensive research efforts were undertaken in the United States, funded by the Department of Defense and NASA. These initiatives led to the development of more rigorous and accurate theoretical bounds, particularly for isotropic composites. The work culminated in the establishment of the Hashin–Shtrikman bounds [1], which offer a more precise estimation of the composite modulus compared to earlier models like Voigt–Reuss model (as illustrated in Figure 3.4). As illustrated in the figure, Hashin–Shtrikman bounds are notably tighter, thereby significantly reducing the safety factor required in engineering designs without compromising the structural integrity and safety of the materials. This advancement marked a critical step forward in optimizing the performance and reliability of composite materials used in high‐stakes applications, particularly in aerospace engineering.

3.3.2 Need for Homogenization Method

Assume that we apply classical laminate theory to perform stress analysis on the entire composite material. This approach allows us to calculate the strain and stress distribution in each reinforcing plate. The resulting stress values represent the average stress, assuming that the reinforced plate is a homogeneous equivalent rather than a composite material. Consequently, the stress experienced by the reinforcing plate fibers and the matrix will differ significantly.

Even if the average stress has been calculated, what will be the actual stress distribution? It remains uncertain whether the calculated average stress accurately reflects the true conditions. When considering cutting reinforced plates or other material processing, understanding this average stress alone is insufficient for formulating the mechanics of processing. If we cannot determine the actual stress within the matrix or fibers themselves, our analysis is incomplete.

Moreover, the theories mentioned earlier fall short of addressing questions about the impact of changes in fiber cross‐sectional shape. Suppose we consider reinforcing fibers with a circular cross‐section (as shown in Figure 3.5), combining fibers of different sizes. Given the arbitrary nature of their arrangement, classical composite mechanics fall short of fully accounting for the effects of this arrangement on the overall structure.

Is there a method that can thoroughly explain the conventional dynamical model while addressing the aforementioned questions? One widely used approach today is homogenization, which has been extensively studied in the field of applied mathematics.

3.3.3 Microstructure with Periodicity

In general composite materials, replicating a perfectly periodic microstructure is practically impossible due to manufacturing constraints. However, for creating a mathematical dynamic model, we can assume approximate periodicity to construct an idealized model.

To target various composite materials, we can abstractly assume a microstructure with periodicity in the vicinity of arbitrary points. By doing so, we can consider points A and B with slightly different periods and microstructures, allowing for the modeling of composite materials with varying properties at different points.

To express this periodicity and fine structure, we introduce the local coordinate y within the fine structure (as illustrated in Figure 3.6). If the general coordinate representing a composite material structure is denoted as x, then the two coordinates x and y can be expressed using parameters ω that represent the scaling for factoring the actual size of the microstructure, establishing the following relationship:

(3.7)

The scaling serves to unitarize the problem.

As shown in the example in Figure 3.6, when articulated through the unit cell model, the smallest representative and ergodic microstructure can be characterized within a domain of 0.4 mm (400 μm). Specifically, let the macroscopic structure be defined by the coordinate x = 400 μm. Correspondingly, the microstructure's local coordinate system, denoted by y, is normalized to unity. When the size of the macrostructure is significantly larger than the microstructure, we can consider the limit as ω → 0. The homogenization method assumes a periodic fine structure and mathematically explores the behavior of the composite material as ω → 0.

3.3.4 Principles of Virtual Work

Here, the superscript indices represent the microstructure. The functions required for such a mechanical formulation use the global coordinate x, representing the entire structure, as a variable. Simultaneously, due to the fine structure, these functions depend on the coordinate y of the unit cell model that represents the fine structure. Therefore, for example, the displacement u and the elastic tensor E can be expressed as the functions of both x and y.

The derivation principle of the homogenization method is employed for the evaluation and design of porous structures, treating voids as materials with infinitesimal properties.

Furthermore, numerical methods used in CAE combined with the homogenization method enable the analysis of microstructures with complex topologies. To provide the reader with a practical understanding of the homogenization method, we will discuss its application with the finite element method for evaluating the mechanical and thermal properties in the following sections.

3.3.5 Approximation by Finite Element Method

Numerical homogenization seeks to derive macroscopic material properties from microscopic behaviors, thereby facilitating the modeling of porous structures on a practical scale. Central to this process is the use of the finite element method to solve the boundary value problems associated with representative volume elements (RVEs). This approach involves dividing the composite's microstructure into a mesh of finite elements, allowing for the detailed analysis of the material's response to unit test loads.

When using the homogenization method for porous structures, it is necessary to calculate the characteristic displacement in the unit cell model and the average displacement u in the homogenized model of the entire structure. This process requires two finite element analyses. If the porous material is not uniform, even in the homogenized model, multiple unit cell models are needed instead of just one.

Approximation using the finite element method on a unit cell model or a homogenized structural model demonstrates convergence, ensuring that the true solution is approached as the elements are further subdivided. Therefore, as long as an appropriate finite element model is used, a solution with high accuracy can be obtained.

Periodic boundary conditions are crucial for accurately representing the behavior of RVEs in numerical homogenization. These types of boundary conditions are employed to simulate an infinite lattice by replicating the unit cell's behavior. Essentially, periodic boundary conditions ensure that the displacements and tractions at the opposite faces of the RVE are identical, thereby mimicking the behavior of an extended ergodic microstructure that makes the material homogeneous.

While the primary application of numerical homogenization has traditionally been to determine the effective elastic tensor of composite materials, the scope of its applicability extends well beyond this. Homogenization techniques can be employed to calculate a variety of physical properties of composites, including thermal conductivity and piezoelectric properties. For instance, in the case of heat conduction, numerical homogenization can be used to derive the effective thermal conductivity tensor by solving heat conduction equations within the RVE and evaluating the resulting heat flux.

Similarly, when analyzing piezoelectric composites, numerical homogenization can be utilized to compute the effective piezoelectric tensor. By applying an electric field and solving the resulting electro‐mechanical problem within the RVE, the effective piezoelectric properties of the composite can be derived.

Chapter 4
Advanced Techniques in Porous Structure Design

4.1 INTRODUCTION

Nowadays, engineering optimization represents a paradigm shift in engineering design, emerging as a simulation‐driven methodology that has significantly advanced the field. This technique serves as a potent instrument for discerning, evaluating, and refining potential design configurations while efficiently eliminating less viable options during the nascent phases of product development. And with the advancement of computational power, numerical methods and computer‐aided manufacturing optimization have thus become indispensable for manufacturers striving to enhance product designs, generate lightweight and manufacturable solutions, and achieve innovation through cost‐effective strategies. The foundation of structural optimization lies in the principles of mathematical optimization and computational simulations, enabling engineers and designers to exploit mathematical rigor and physical laws to optimize structures and materials with unparalleled precision and efficiency.

The transformative power of structural optimization is particularly evident in its ability to accelerate the design process. Historically, the development of new products was a time‐intensive and iterative endeavor, characterized by the construction, testing, and subsequent modification of prototypes until a satisfactory design was realized. This conventional approach not only consumed substantial time and resources but also generated considerable material waste. In contrast, engineering optimization offers a more streamlined and efficient alternative. By leveraging advanced computer‐aided design (CAD) software and simulation tools, engineers can accurately model the structural behavior of a product under various loading conditions and constraints, thereby efficiently replicating real‐world scenarios within a virtual environment.

The process of numerical optimization is a search algorithm by nature, with optimization algorithms playing a central role in refining the design. These algorithms adjust the design parameters, systematically exploring the design space to identify configurations that meet predefined performance criteria. For instance, a common objective in structural optimization is to minimize the weight of a component while ensuring that it maintains sufficient strength to withstand operational conditions. Other objectives might include reducing material usage, enhancing durability, or improving manufacturability.

In addition to its efficiency, engineering optimization is also highly adaptable and can incorporate multidisciplinary problems, making it applicable across a broad spectrum of industries and design challenges. Whether in aerospace, automotive, civil engineering, or consumer products, optimization can be tailored to meet the specific requirements of each application. For example, in the aerospace industry, where weight reduction is a critical concern, optimization is used extensively to design lightweight components that meet stringent performance and safety standards. Similarly, in the automotive industry, optimization helps engineers design parts that are both lightweight and durable, contributing to improved fuel efficiency and vehicle performance. In civil engineering, structural optimization is applied to the design of bridges, buildings, and other infrastructure projects, ensuring that these structures are both safe and cost‐effective.

The principles of computational simulations that underpin structural optimization are grounded in the mathematical modeling of physical phenomena.

The analysis of physical phenomena often involves discretization methods, where the domain of the phenomenon is divided into smaller, semi‐regular representations. These smaller sections can be solved simultaneously or cluster by cluster, depending on the chosen numerical method, the available computational power, and the specific physics of the problem. Common discretization methods include finite difference methods, finite element methods, boundary element methods, and finite volume methods.

Additionally, there have been attempts to utilize mesh‐free methods, although these approaches face significant challenges. Machine learning has also entered the realm of simulation and analysis due to its mathematical similarities with traditional discretization methods. Neural networks, for instance, share a structural resemblance to numerical methods for solving partial differential equations (PDEs) and have been adapted to tackle such problems effectively.

It is important to note that many of the currently used topological discretization techniques trace their origins back to mathematical discretization methods for physical and mathematical problems, such as series expansions. These foundational methods have shaped the development of modern approaches to modeling and solving complex phenomena.

In this book, the method used to simulate and perform optimization is the finite element method. Finite element analysis (FEA) stands as one of the main cornerstones for numerical techniques in engineering, enabling the detailed examination of complex structures by discretizing them into a mesh of smaller, interconnected elements. Each of these elements serves as a localized domain within which the mathematical equations governing physical phenomena—such as deformation, stress, and strain—are solved. Through this process, FEA can successfully provide a comprehensive simulation of how an entire structure responds to various external fields and boundary conditions, offering engineers a profound understanding of structural behavior under a myriad of scenarios.

The optimization process commences with valid methodology using a finite element model representing the product or structure under consideration. This model is a detailed digital representation encapsulating all critical geometric features and material properties that define the design's essence. The next step involves the articulation of the objective function, a mathematical expression that quantifies the design goals, such as minimizing mass, maximizing stiffness, or enhancing durability. In parallel, engineers define constraints to ensure that the optimization adheres to essential requirements, which may include safety factors, material limits, or manufacturability constraints.

Once the finite element model, objective function, and constraints are established, the optimization process unfolds through an iterative cycle. During this phase, various design parameters—such as dimensions, material distributions, or geometric configurations—are systematically adjusted. Following each adjustment, the finite element model undergoes reanalysis to ascertain the impact of these changes on the overall performance metrics. Optimization algorithms, functioning in tandem with the finite element solver, diligently search for the optimal set of design variables that fulfill the specified objectives while satisfying all imposed constraints. This iterative refinement continues until an optimal design configuration is achieved, one that offers the best possible balance between competing criteria.

The choice of optimization algorithms is diverse, with each type tailored to address different classes of design problems. Among the most commonly employed algorithms are gradient‐based methods, metaheuristic algorithms [1, 2], and biologically inspired methods [3]. Gradient‐based methods are well‐suited for optimization problems characterized by smooth, continuous design spaces. These algorithms leverage the gradients of the objective function with respect to the design variables, enabling efficient navigation through the design space toward an optimal solution. However, they are most effective in scenarios where the design space is well‐behaved and lacks significant discontinuities or local extrema.

Metaheuristic algorithms somehow are more adept at tackling complex design spaces that may be discrete, highly non‐linear, or riddled with local optima, for example. Genetic algorithms operate on a population of potential solutions, iteratively evolving them through processes analogous to mutation, crossover, and selection. This stochastic approach enables genetic algorithms to explore a wider design space, making them especially useful for problems with discrete design variables or complex, rugged solution landscapes. However, most metaheuristic algorithms require careful tuning of parameters to match the mathematical function being optimized. Additionally, these algorithms can be computationally expensive, which may limit their efficiency in certain applications.

The integration of these optimization algorithms with FEA enables engineers to address a wide array of design challenges with precision and efficiency.

Moreover, the synergy between FEA and optimization extends beyond traditional physical problems to encompass advanced materials and innovative manufacturing techniques. In the realm of additive manufacturing, for example, optimization algorithms are used to design lattice structures that are both lightweight and strong, tailored to the unique capabilities of three‐dimensional (3D) printing technologies.

Engineering optimization proves particularly beneficial in addressing multi‐objective problems, where design criteria often conflict. For instance, in the aerospace sector, engineers may seek to reduce both the weight and drag of an aircraft to boost fuel efficiency. However, these objectives can be at odds with one another, necessitating a careful balance. Structural optimization excels in navigating these complex trade‐offs, enabling the identification of Pareto‐optimal solutions—designs that represent the best possible compromises between competing objectives. This capability to explore and balance multiple design possibilities is a significant advantage, allowing engineers to make informed, strategic decisions about the final design configuration.

Beyond its application in achieving lightweight structures, structural optimization also plays a crucial role in the manufacturing process. While lightweight designs are often advantageous, they can also present manufacturing challenges due to their intricate geometries and the need for specialized materials and techniques. Engineering optimization bridges the gap between design and manufacturing by ensuring that optimized designs are not only efficient and lightweight but also manufacturable. Engineers can integrate manufacturing constraints—such as material thickness limitations, moldability considerations, or specific assembly methods—directly into the optimization process. By doing so, they mitigate the risk of generating designs that, while theoretically optimal, might be impractical or prohibitively expensive to produce.

Optimization also greatly enhances the practice of virtual prototyping, which has become an integral part of modern product development. Traditionally, product development involved creating physical prototypes for testing and validation—a process that could be both time‐consuming and costly. Optimization, however, allows for the creation of virtual prototypes that can be subjected to a comprehensive suite of simulated tests, including structural, thermal, and fluid dynamics analyses. Optimization‐guided virtual prototyping provides a detailed understanding of the design's performance across a range of scenarios, enabling engineers to make informed adjustments well before any physical prototype is built. This approach not only conserves resources but also significantly accelerates the product development cycle, allowing for faster time to market.

As we explore further, we encounter both parametric and non‐parametric approaches within the realm of engineering optimization, especially structural optimization. Parametric optimization relies on specific design parameters and mathematical models to refine a given design, allowing for control over the optimization process and attaining the design according to that parameter. In contrast, non‐parametric optimization employs more generalized methods that can adapt to a wider range of design variables such that all the discretized elements in the design domain are addressed in optimization. Each of these approaches offers unique advantages and is suited to different types of optimization problems. In the following sections, these methodologies are discussed.

4.2 PARAMETRIC OPTIMIZATION

Parametric optimization is a mathematical approach to optimizing a system or structure by varying one or more parameters within a predefined range to achieve the best performance according to specific objectives. In engineering, this technique is widely applied to enhance the efficiency, strength, or functionality of designs. Parameters may include geometric dimensions, material properties, boundary conditions, or loading configurations. For instance, in structural optimization, parameters such as beam thickness, cross‐sectional area, or material density can be adjusted to minimize weight while maximizing mechanical strength or stiffness. The optimization problem is typically defined by an objective function, such as minimizing stress or maximizing thermal conductivity, subject to constraints like allowable deformation or manufacturing limitations. By systematically analyzing how changes in parameters affect performance, engineers can identify optimal configurations that balance competing requirements, such as cost, durability, and functionality.

Parametric optimization involves finding the optimal values of parameters p = {p1, p2,… , pn} that minimize or maximize an objective function f(p), subjected to the constraints of gi(p) ≤ 0 and hi(p) = 0. Mathematically, this can be expressed as

(4.1)

For example, consider the parametric optimization problem of a cantilever beam. The process typically begins by selecting the initial shape, and then applying optimization to adjust a parameter within the shape. In the case of a rectangular cross‐section cantilever beam, consider a beam subjected to a force F at its free end. The objective is to minimize the deflection δ, which depends on the beam's thickness t. The deflection is given by

(4.2)

where L is the beam length, and E is Young's modulus. I is the 2nd moment of area (i.e., the moment of inertia), and it takes the form of

(4.3)

where w is the width of the beam. By varying the thickness t, the optimal value can be identified to minimize deflection while adhering to manufacturing or material constraints. This variation in t represents a systematic exploration of how deflection behaves as a function of thickness, ultimately allowing us to determine the most effective value. For this example, consider a cantilever beam with a square cross‐section, a length of 10 m, a width of 0.1 m, a Young's modulus of 2 × 109 Pa, and a force of 1 Newton applied at its free end. By varying the thickness from 0.01 to 0.1 m and plotting the resulting deflection, we can observe the behavior of the deflection function. The results of the deflection versus the thickness are depicted in Figure 4.1.

To replicate this analysis, the reader can execute the accompanying Python code, which generates the results as illustrated in Figure 4.1. This practical implementation provides insights into the interplay between structural geometry and deflection, a fundamental concept in engineering design.

The deflection function behavior in Figure 4.1 shows convexity in the thickness belonging to the interval [0.01, 0.1]. This convexity indicates that the optimization process, aimed at minimizing deflection, will achieve a unique global minimum within this specified bound. The best result is attained at a thickness of 0.1 m. Given the convexity of the deflection function within the specified bounds, it is feasible to directly implement optimization techniques to achieve optimal results, bypassing the need for repetitive evaluations and extensive plotting of the entire function. One effective method for this optimization is the Lagrangian approach, which incorporates constraints into the objective function [4, 5]. By introducing Lagrange multipliers, the optimization problem can be reformulated to find the stationary points of the Lagrangian, thus ensuring that the global minimum is attained while satisfying any imposed constraints. This approach significantly enhances computational efficiency and precision in the optimization process. Using this Python code, we implement the optimization, and the results are presented in Figure 4.2.

4.3 NON‐PARAMETRIC OPTIMIZATION

Non‐parametric optimization represents an indispensable dimension of contemporary structural and multiphysics design, driving the evolution of optimal configurations that meet stringent performance criteria while effectively navigating the multifaceted challenges posed by real‐world constraints. Diverging from traditional parametric methodologies, non‐parametric optimization focuses on the intricate manipulation of structural geometry and topology by utilizing the discretization methods. This paradigm shift is exemplified by two principal methodologies: shape optimization and topology optimization. Shape optimization centers on the refinement of a structure's boundaries to satisfy the design problem by updating the element on the boundary shape while maintaining the internal material distribution.

On the other hand, topology optimization addresses the whole elements in the design domain. The transformative potential of non‐parametric optimization lies in its departure from explicit parameterization. Unlike traditional methods, which rely on predefined design parameters, non‐parametric optimization harnesses the intrinsic geometry and topology of the structure as the primary drivers of the optimization process. This approach imbues the resulting designs with fluidity and adaptability that resonates with the complexity of real‐world scenarios, often yielding solutions that surpass the capabilities of parametric counterparts.

To facilitate this optimization, the design domain is discretized using numerical methods such as the finite element method. Discretization converts the continuous structure into a discrete form, comprising a multitude of finite elements, each associated with a design variable. These design variables represent the material density or presence within each element, effectively determining whether the material should be retained, reduced, or eliminated in a particular region. The iterative nature of the topology optimization process involves updating these design variables at each iteration step, guided by the objective function and constraints.

A key aspect of topology optimization is its ability to address complex engineering challenges, such as maximizing structural stiffness, minimizing weight, or optimizing thermal conductivity. The objective function is defined based on the specific performance criterion of interest, and the optimization algorithm seeks to find the optimal material distribution that extremizes this objective while adhering to constraints such as maximum allowable stress, displacement, or volume fraction.

For instance, in a heat maximization problem, the objective function might be to maximize the thermal conductivity of a structure, subject to constraints on material usage and geometric boundaries. The design domain is discretized, and the topology optimization algorithm iteratively adjusts the material distribution to identify the optimal configuration that achieves the desired thermal performance. The resulting design may exhibit a complex and non‐intuitive topology, reflecting the intricate interplay between thermal conductivity and material distribution (as illustrated in Figure 4.3).

The power of topology optimization lies in its ability to generate complex, innovative, and efficient designs that are tailored to specific engineering challenges. By allowing the material to be freely redistributed within the design domain, topology optimization can uncover novel structural configurations that would be difficult, if not impossible, to conceive using traditional design methods. These designs often feature intricate internal geometries, optimized load paths, and minimized stress concentrations, all of which contribute to enhanced performance and reduced material usage.

Moreover, the integration of non‐parametric optimization techniques into the design process offers significant advantages in terms of flexibility and adaptability. Unlike parametric methods, which are constrained by predefined design variables and relationships, non‐parametric optimization allows for a more organic and unconstrained exploration of the design space. This flexibility is particularly valuable in scenarios where the design objectives and constraints are complex or where the optimal solution is not readily apparent.

Historically, topology optimization originated as a conceptual framework introduced by J. Maxwell [7], who laid the foundational ideas of discretizing the structure as domains to small segments using the energy principle. This discretization method may not be considered new, as this era has witnessed significant advancements in the use of series to solve engineering problems. However, the pioneering work of Maxwell opened the door to variational principles, which laid the foundation for the development of widely used computational methods such as the finite element and finite volume methods. Furthermore, the discretization approach, later known as Maxwell's lemma, intrigued both mathematicians and engineers, prompting its application in optimizing complex structures that would otherwise take an extensive amount of time to solve using series. It was not until the groundbreaking contributions of A.G.M. Michell [8] that the field experienced a significant advancement. Michell's seminal work established a practical methodology for applying mathematical optimization principles to the design of structural architectures, effectively bridging the gap between theoretical concepts and practical engineering applications. His approach provided a systematic and quantifiable means of determining the most efficient structural layouts, minimizing material usage while maximizing performance. Michell's innovative techniques catalyzed further research, leading to the evolution and refinement of topology optimization methods. These advancements have since been integrated into a wide spectrum of engineering and design disciplines, yielding highly efficient and optimized structural systems. The foundational work of Michell has inspired a myriad of subsequent studies, each contributing to the expansion and enhancement of topology optimization, solidifying its role as a critical tool in modern engineering design [9–12],

The advent of modern topology optimization was fundamentally shaped by the integration of homogenization techniques, which facilitated the confluence of advanced mathematical concepts and contemporary computational methods. This integration was exemplified by the development of the discretized continuous optimality criterion method [13], which subsequently laid the groundwork for the solid isotropic material with penalization (SIMP) approach. The SIMP method has been notably applied to the maximization of thermal performance, as demonstrated in the research conducted by Joo et al. [14]. Their investigation focused on optimizing heat sink designs using the SIMP approach, addressing the challenge of local minima by incorporating a surrogate model to enhance the optimization efficacy.

In contrast, Koga et al. [15] employed an unconventional approach to heat sink designs by focusing on the internal channel configuration within a metallic block. Their methodology utilized a steady‐state flow objective function and incorporated Stokes flow principles to optimize the internal structure, thus providing an innovative alternative to traditional heat sink design approaches.

Their study, employing the SIMP method, considered both steady‐state conduction and convective heat transfer from side surfaces, thereby presenting a holistic approach to thermal management. This body of research underscores the ongoing refinement and application of topology optimization techniques in developing highly efficient and sophisticated thermal management solutions.

4.4 MULTIPHYSICS TOPOLOGY OPTIMIZATION FUNDAMENTALS

Topology optimization shares conceptual underpinnings with traditional parametric optimization methodologies. Optimization, in essence, involves identifying extrema within a mathematically formulated problem, which encompasses variables, constraints, and an objective function. Variables determine the structure of the objective function, the choice of optimization techniques, and any constraints applied. Constraints, when incorporated into the optimization framework, delineate the feasible solution space, guiding the optimization process toward feasible solutions. The objective function, assuming that it exhibits a smooth behavior over the design space, is subjected to the extremum search process.

In optimization, the goal is to explore the design space defined by the objective function using various search algorithms to locate the extrema of the function concerning the design variables. These design variables are integral to shaping the objective function and thus directly influence the optimization outcome.

In contrast, non‐parametric optimization methods, such as topology optimization, employ discretized representations that mitigate the impact of changes in the objective function on the design's topological flexibility. This is due to the implicit nature of how topology is represented within the objective function, allowing for a broader range of design alterations without significantly constraining the topological freedom of the solution space.

The objective function often undergoes significant modifications to achieve desirable extrema. Additionally, the degree of convexity of the objective function is a crucial factor in determining the most optimal solution, which is further elucidated through sensitivity analysis. Greater convexity typically increases the likelihood of identifying global extrema. Consequently, the minimum value is typically obtained after “sufficient trials,” with the number of trials being proportional to the convexity of the function.

To find the extremum of a function, direct search algorithms are commonly utilized, commencing from an initial point characterized by estimated strong convexity. To ensure robustness, constraints are typically employed to delineate the search domain. These constraints, whether defined by equality or inequality conditions, are crucial for restricting the design to a “feasible search space.” Search algorithms initiate from a designated starting point, progressing with a controlled step size (ε), as illustrated in Figure 4.5.

Optimizing a function under specific constraints necessitates achieving what is termed as the feasibility of the design problem. Constraints on the extremum can be effectively managed by reformulating the problem into an unconstrained optimization framework through the application of penalty functions. This approach allows for the transformation of constrained optimization into a more manageable form by penalizing deviations from the constraints within the objective function.

(4.13)

The penalty parameter, denoted as (β), plays a critical role in the optimization process. Selecting appropriate penalty parameters is essential for the problem's formulation. Additionally, the Lagrangian function is an effective approach for addressing constraint problems, achieved by introducing Lagrange multipliers, denoted as (ℓ).

(4.14)

The constraint type plays an important role in solving the optimization problem. In general, the constraints are classified as: equality constraints (g(x) = 0) and non‐equality constraints (g(x) < 0). In this work, the Lagrange method with equality constraints is adopted as the main method of updating the design variables. Optimizing with search Lagrangian is considerably fast, but the search speed will degrade with increasing the problem resolution (i.e., the number of elements in both the macro [ΩdM] and micro [Ωdm] design domains).

Several techniques exist to accelerate the process by adaptively adjusting the search steps based on statistical measures, although this falls outside the scope of this paper. Additionally, metaheuristic optimization methods [17] do not require sensitivity analysis. However, for topology optimization, the metaheuristic approach incurs significant computational costs, making it unsuitable for multi‐scale and multiphysics topology optimization problems.

In general, multi‐objective optimization problems yield multiple solutions. Addressing a specific multi‐objective function formulation often necessitates satisfying conditions for Pareto optimality. Ideally, one might identify a dominant solution, which is defined as being superior to all other solutions with respect to at least one objective function while being at least as good as the remaining solutions concerning the other objectives. When a solution fulfills these criteria, it is termed a Pareto optimal solution. The general formulation of multi‐objective optimization is represented in Eq. (4.15).

A prevalent approach in multi‐objective optimization is to reformulate the problem as a summation of individual objective functions. In this study, the multi‐objective problem is simplified to a single composite function representing the weighted sum of the various objectives. The weights for this summation are predetermined prior to the optimization process.

(4.16)

The solution's fitness and prioritization are governed by the weight assigned to the objective function (wi). A higher weight indicates greater importance for that objective function compared to others with smaller weights, as illustrated in Figure 4.6.

In practical scenarios, achieving feasible design variables for all objective functions may not always be possible. This limitation implies that the Pareto front, which represents the set of non‐dominated solutions in multi‐objective optimization, may not be accurately represented across all objectives. Consequently, the resulting solutions could fail to satisfy the criteria for a proper Pareto optimal solution. Among these, normalization stands out as a particularly reliable approach. This technique ensures that no single objective function dominates the optimization process solely due to its initial scale or value. In this study, we utilize the goal programming method, which seeks to minimize the deviation of the solution vector from the ideal values of each objective function. The goal programming approach identifies solutions along the Pareto front that best satisfy the specified weighting criteria. 4.5 TOPOLOGY OPTIMIZATION METHODOLOGIES

Topology optimization has seen considerable progress in recent years and remains a vibrant research field, largely propelled by advancements in CAD and the rapid development of additive manufacturing technologies. This optimization approach allows for the use of mathematical search algorithms to iteratively refine the design domain and identify the optimal configuration. A variety of methodologies for topology optimization have been proposed in the literature, with the SIMP method, the evolutionary structural optimization (ESO) method, and the metaheuristic structure binary‐distribution (MSB) method being the most widely discussed. Each of these methods will be explored in detail.

4.5.1 Solid Isotropic Materials with Penalization Method

The SIMP method for topology optimization involves discretizing the design domain to identify a smooth, optimal structure. Derived from homogenization theory [18], the SIMP method assumes that the material consists of isotropically distributed density fields, referred to as artificial densities. To enforce binarization of the design domain—essentially determining whether a material exists at a given location—these artificial densities must be penalized using a power law. This penalty encourages the design domain to adopt discrete material states. Consequently, material properties are interpolated based on the penalized density field within the discretized domain. The optimization problem is then formulated as maximizing or minimizing the objective function, which incorporates the density field through the interpolation scheme, with respect to the design variables of the density field, as shown in Eq. (4.19)

(4.19)

where X(x)| is the cost function, and x is the design variables that represent SIMP as the artificial isotropic density. As previously noted, the SIMP method is favored for its robustness in structural optimization. However, excessively high penalization values can compromise the convexity of the objective function, potentially leading the optimization process to converge on local minima rather than the global optimum. In the current framework, several challenges arise, notably checkerboard patterns and non‐existence issues. The latter, which pertains to mesh dependency, often results in the introduction of zero‐density elements, a phenomenon driven by the algorithm's attempt to minimize the objective function.

To address this, the relaxation principle is employed. This principle involves adjusting the density function to incorporate a so‐called gray region, thereby mitigating the occurrence of zero‐density elements and improving solution robustness. (x ∈ (0, 1]). The application of heuristic algorithms with appropriate constraints is often employed to address the challenges of topology optimization, particularly in mitigating issues such as checkerboarding. Checkerboarding arises as a consequence of discretization and is inherently linked to the heuristic approach used to identify optimal solutions. This issue manifests when design elements are constrained to binary states—either fully present or completely absent—resulting in discontinuous sensitivity variations across the finite element mesh. Consequently, small changes in the presence of an element can lead to abrupt changes in the objective function values due to the discretization process.

Checkerboarding is especially prevalent with low‐order finite elements, where the coarse resolution exacerbates the problem. While increasing the order of the elements, that is, the number of nodes per element, might alleviate checkerboarding, it incurs a substantial increase in computational cost. Alternatively, enhancing the resolution of the design domain by increasing the number of elements can reduce the checkerboard patterns significantly, yet this also results in a considerable rise in computational demand.

To address these issues more effectively, one approach is to implement sensitivity filtering techniques. By averaging the sensitivities of neighboring elements—those in proximity to the element in question—through mesh‐independent filtering schemes, the continuity of sensitivity variations is improved, thereby reducing the appearance of checkerboarding. This method helps balance the trade‐offs between solution accuracy and computational efficiency.

4.5.2 Evolutionary Structural Optimization Method

The optimization process in topology design is inherently influenced by the initial configuration of design variables, which promotes the adoption of binarization techniques. In these approaches, the design domain is initially defined with binary values—either 0 or 1—and remains updated throughout the optimization process until the final design is achieved. This strategy aligns with the practice of binarized topology optimization methods, such as the ESO method, which has gained significant traction in recent years [19–21].

ESO operates on the principle of binarizing the initial design domain, wherein design variables are set to binary states from the outset. The method utilizes a procedure that involves progressively zeroing out the central element values within a periodic structure. This approach effectively triggers an inverse homogenization process, where the material is systematically removed based on the performance criteria. The overarching doctrine of ESO is to achieve a clear delineation between materials and voids, optimizing the structure by removing inefficient material regions while maintaining the overall structural integrity. This binarization process facilitates a straightforward and computationally efficient means of arriving at optimized structural designs [22, 23].

Implementing ESO under these specified conditions results in a uniform design distribution while mitigating the risk of local minima. ESO achieves this by progressively addressing nearly the entire design domain and striving to minimize the volume to identify an optimal binary topology.

The fundamental principle of ESO revolves around the direct elimination of “inefficient materials” to progressively shape the structure into an optimal design. Originally introduced by Min and Steven [19], ESO leverages the sensitivity of the cost function to guide updates to the design variables. This approach systematically refines the material distribution based on its effectiveness, ensuring that the final design is optimized for performance and material efficiency [24]. Updating depends on the element's sensitivity number αi, as shown in Eq. (4.20):

(4.20)

In essence, ESO focuses on eliminating redundant materials, identified by their low sensitivity values. This methodology is constrained to the removal of materials only, meaning that once materials are discarded, they cannot be reintroduced. Consequently, an initially oversized design domain is crucial to guarantee that the final design encompasses all potential configurations.

To address this limitation, the bi‐directional evolutionary structural optimization (BESO) method was developed [25]. BESO enhances the robustness of ESO by allowing both the addition and removal of materials within the design domain. This bi‐directional approach improves the overall flexibility of the optimization process, thereby reducing the likelihood of converging on local extrema and ensuring a more comprehensive exploration of the design space.

4.5.3 Metaheuristic Structure Binary‐Distribution Method

The MSB method, introduced by Al‐Ali et al. [6], is designed to expedite the resolution of topology optimization problems while mitigating the risk of encountering local minima. This methodology integrates YUKI's hybrid metaheuristic optimization with gradient descent techniques. To provide clarity, we will first present a concise overview of the YUKI algorithm, followed by a detailed exposition of the MSB method's formulations.

The YUKI algorithm is a novel metaheuristic optimization method designed to find optimal solutions with minimal computational effort. It achieves this through three key features. The first characteristic of the YUKI optimization algorithm is its use of the best solutions from a given iteration (i) as the starting point for finding improved solutions in the subsequent iteration (i + 1).

The YUKI algorithm integrates the notion of “Best Points” to represent the personal best solutions within the optimization process. By harnessing the collective intelligence of all points within the swarm, each individual point effectively navigates the search space and refines its best solutions. This collective approach enhances the global search capability, allowing the swarm to continuously adapt and improve. In each iteration, the algorithm evaluates the population of solutions and utilizes a vector to monitor the values associated with the optimal fitness criteria—such as minimizing thermal compliance in the current context. The Best Points accumulated throughout the iterations are then employed to determine a singular representative point, termed the “MeanBest.” This MeanBest point serves as the centroid of the cluster of optimal solutions, with its value computed as the average of the identified Best Points. This method ensures a balanced and accurate representation of the optimal solution, thereby improving the efficacy of the optimization process.

The YUKI algorithm initiates by defining a local search domain centered around the optimal solution identified up to that point. The extent of this search domain is dictated by the distance between this solution and the MeanBest point. This methodology encompasses several distinctive attributes:

  1. 1. Adaptive Search Domain: As solutions progress toward the optimal, the local search domain contracts, reflecting increased convergence and refinement in the search.
  2. 2. Maintained Global Coverage: The distance between Best Points is preserved sufficiently large, ensuring comprehensive global search coverage and mitigating the risk of premature convergence to local minima.
  3. 3. Dynamic Adjustment: The search domain size is dynamically adjusted based on the discovery of new optimal solutions within the local area, allowing for expansion of the search space as needed.
  4. 4. Continuous Update: Throughout the optimization process, if no new optimal solution is identified, the MeanBest is regularly updated, facilitating the generation of various search domain sizes and maintaining search dynamism.
  5. 5. Variable Sensitivity: The algorithm's effectiveness is contingent on the global search space size rather than individual design variables. Consequently, the local search domain may exhibit significant variation in size across different dimensions, reflecting the sensitivity of each variable and ensuring that the algorithm adapts to the distinct characteristics of each design variable.

The local boundaries are calculated by the following expressions:

(4.21)
(4.22)
(4.23)

where xbest represents the variable value associated with the best fitness value discovered to date. xMeanBest denotes the average of the “Best Points” vector. LT and LB refer to the local upper and lower boundaries of the search space, respectively.

The second characteristic of the YUKI algorithm is the division of the search space (Ω). The population (i.e., the design variables) is divided into two segments. One segment is tasked with exploring areas outside the local search region (ΩEXP), while the other focuses on searching within the local search region (ΩLocal).

The population size within each segment of the search space is adjusted in a linear fashion. Initially, the algorithm allocates a substantial proportion of the population to exploration, facilitating a broad search of the design space. As iterations advance, the focus gradually shifts toward intensifying the search within the local vicinity of promising solutions. This transition is governed by a probabilistic mechanism: A random value is generated between 0 and 1 and compared against a threshold value derived from a specific expression. This comparison determines the extent of focus on exploration versus exploitation within the local search area, thereby optimizing the balance between global exploration and local refinement [26].

(4.24)

where rand is the random value between 0 and 1, and EXP is the exploration parameter of the YUKI algorithm, which assigns a percentage of the population to search outside the local search area. In contrast to other metaheuristic optimization algorithms, the YUKI algorithm excels in efficiently managing computational resources. This is achieved by concentrating efforts on areas with the most promising solutions while concurrently maintaining a broad scan of other regions.

A distinctive feature of the YUKI algorithm is its ability to conduct a comprehensive examination of the focus area through extensive exploration. Initially, it generates a random distribution of points within the local search domain. These points are then employed to probe potential solutions beyond the confines of the local search area. The exploration is strategically directed toward regions further from the MeanBest, a reference point defined by the position of the MeanBest, as specified in Eq. (4.25). This approach ensures that the search dynamically adapts, enhancing the likelihood of discovering optimal solutions.

4.6 SHAPE OPTIMIZATION

Shape optimization is a subset of non‐parametric optimization that focuses on modifying the boundaries of the design domain to achieve an optimal configuration. Unlike parametric optimization, which relies on predefined variables, shape optimization updates the geometry directly, allowing for greater design flexibility. Key methods in this field include the level‐set method, the H1 gradient method, and the phase‐field method (PFM). These methods are discussed in detail in the following subsections.

4.6.1 Level‐Set Method

The level‐set method, akin to ESO and MSB, employs binarized design variables. Originally conceived by Dervieux and Thomasset [27] for studying multi‐phase flows, this methodology is fundamentally a non‐parametric technique that implicitly represents shapes. Its formulation and application were initially explored in the context of fluid interfaces [28, 29]. The term “level set” itself was coined later by Osher and Sethian [30], who introduced complex topological notations to the method.

The level‐set method has found extensive applications in segmentation and object shape identification within the realm of computer vision [31]. Initially constrained by the computational limitations of its time, due to its implicit nature and significant resource demands, the method's viability was restricted. However, advancements in computational power have alleviated these constraints, allowing for the method's application to more computationally intensive problems.

Subsequent developments led to the successful use of the level‐set method in stress minimization problems by Sethian and Wiegmann [32], marking a significant shift toward its application in moderately computationally demanding tasks such as structural strain energy minimization [33, 34]. This evolution underscores the method's adaptability and efficacy in addressing complex optimization challenges.

In shape optimization, the level set provides an implicit representation of the transformation of the decision variables x, which are part of the cost function, within a domain defined by the level‐set function φ. This representation specifically addresses a level ℓ, at which the function has a neutral sign. Values above this level yield a positive sign, while those below it result in a negative sign for the design variable.

(4.29)

As shown in Figure 4.7, the representation of the curve outline changes at the intersection of the measuring level of the level set and the functional surface.

The level‐set method offers a transformative approach to representing shapes within cost functions, generalizing the shape representation such that perturbations at specific points above or below the level set do not significantly impact the overall shape. This robustness addresses numerical instabilities inherent in earlier formulations, particularly in the abstract form of the level‐set method where shape derivatives are considered without relying on parametrized bi‐directional updates or topology‐derivative regulation factors [35].

In the context of topology optimization using the level‐set method, the problem is typically framed as a single material‐void scenario. The level‐set representation is effectively captured by the signed distance function, which is articulated mathematically in Eq. (4.30).

(4.30)

where ℓ is the level plane that the function is evaluated around. Here the intersection boundaries of the function and the level plane are the boundaries to be evaluated for. The topology optimization problem necessitates the binary material representation to have 1 for the material existence and 0 for the void. This representation can ease the choice of ℓ to be 0, and this complies with Osher notation [30].

In general, level set‐based topology optimization does not operate directly on the cost function X but instead focuses on the function φ. Since φ represents a surface, it simplifies the evolution process from a mathematical standpoint. However, as previously mentioned, the implicit representation of φ incurs additional computational costs, requiring updates to all decision variables x in ℝ2.

Thus, the level‐set function is constructed under the premise of evolving the curve through the orthogonal progression of the design variable along the level‐set plane. The evolution proceeds in the form of

The boundaries are subsequently updated using optimization methods, such as the forward Euler scheme. This method incorporates the Heaviside function, which assigns a value of one when the level‐set function is positive or zero, and a value of zero in void regions.
