(4.39)

The level‐set method has been introduced as a technique that avoids mesh‐dependent issues such as checkerboard patterns [36] and grayscale artifacts [37]. However, as an evolutionary approach, the function parameters are problem‐dependent.

In other words, it is important to select a suitable initial value of φ and the normal velocity v. The evolution of boundaries in topology optimization via the level‐set method may exhibit insufficient sensitivity to local variations within the optimization loop, potentially leading to the convergence toward local minima.

This limitation arises from the method's inherent constraint in dynamically creating new boundaries within solid regions, as well as its challenge in accommodating changes distant from the initial boundaries. Several strategies have been proposed to address these limitations, including the incorporation of topological derivatives, parameterization of the level‐set function, and the application of regularized Heaviside functions to mitigate numerical instabilities and avoid singularities in the conductivity matrix. The Heaviside function, which assumes a value of one for positive values of the level‐set function and zero for void regions, plays a crucial role in these adjustments. In the current study, the focus remains solely on shape derivatives. Notably, the level‐set method has been extensively applied in multi‐scale topology optimization for both static and dynamic mechanical problems by various researchers [38–40].

4.6.2 H1 Gradient Method

The H1 gradient method is used as a shape optimization technique to refine the microstructure's detailed shape. Originally introduced by Azegami to address domain optimization challenges in boundary value problems [41], this method was later extended by Shimoda et al. through numerical analysis using general‐purpose finite element software [42]. It has since been applied to various structures, including shell frame and fiber orientation [43, 44] problems. More recently, the H1 gradient method has been adapted for shape optimization in multi‐scale structures. For instance, Fujioka et al. proposed a shape optimization approach for microstructures aimed at maximizing the stiffness of a macrostructure in a two‐dimensional elastic body composed of porous materials under area constraints [45]. Additionally, they developed methods focused on enhancing structural strength through shape optimization, specifically for designing porous structures with high strength [46].

4.6.3 Phase‐Field Method

The PFM has emerged as a powerful approach for addressing shape optimization challenges in engineering and design. Initially developed for simulating phase transitions in materials, PFM has been adapted to a broad spectrum of applications, including shape optimization. In this method, the geometry of a design is represented by a continuous phase‐field variable, φ(x) ∈ [0, 1], which smoothly transitions between distinct phases. Typically, φ(x) = 1 denotes the material inclusion, while φ(x) = 0 represents the surrounding void, with a diffuse interface between these regions. This representation eliminates the need for explicit boundary tracking, simplifying the modeling of complex geometries and topological changes. PFM frames the problem as the minimization (or maximization) of an objective functional J(φ, χ), where χ represents state variables governed by physical laws. The evolution of the phase‐field variable is governed by a PDE, which ensures smooth boundary transitions and allows for topological flexibility. The objective functional is augmented with a regularization term based on the Ginzburg–Landau energy to maintain numerical stability and ensure well‐posedness [47–49].

4.7 POROUS STRUCTURAL DESIGN

Over the past few decades, numerous approaches have evolved in the design of porous structures to efficiently achieve robust solutions that meet specific requirements. Prior to the advancements in additive manufacturing, many designs were constrained by the limitations of traditional manufacturing methods, where cost and manufacturability were major factors. This lack of design freedom significantly hindered the development of porous structures, and the concept of designing the pores themselves was often considered impractical. However, the emergence of 3D‐printing technologies has revolutionized this paradigm, offering unprecedented freedom in design and fabrication. As a result, new methods for porous structure design have emerged, enabling more sophisticated and optimized solutions. The following section will explore these innovative methodologies in porous structure design.

4.7.1 Biologically Inspired

The design of porous structures inspired by cellular biological systems draws upon the extraordinary efficiency and functionality observed in nature's lattice architectures. Natural porous structures, such as the trabecular bone in human anatomy or the intricate wings of a dragonfly, exemplify optimized strength‐to‐weight ratios, with resilience to impact. These biological models serve as a valuable source of inspiration for developing engineered porous materials that seek to replicate the advantages found in these natural systems.

The modern process of designing bio‐inspired porous structures often begins with a detailed analysis of existing natural models. A critical step involves extracting microscale design features using advanced imaging techniques such as computed tomography (CT) scans. These scans provide detailed cross‐sectional images of natural porous structures, capturing the intricate micro‐architecture with high precision. The data from CT scans are then used to create a 3D representation of the porous structure, often saved in a format such as stereolithography (STL) files. Once the 3D model is established, the microstructure is chosen and evaluated (as illustrated in Figure 4.8). The advent of 3D‐printing technology has revolutionized the production of these intricate porous structures.

With 3D printing, researchers can fabricate complex, variable‐porosity designs with high precision and accuracy. This technology allows for the creation of structures that would be challenging to achieve using traditional manufacturing methods. The ability to print detailed and customized porous structures directly from digital models significantly enhances the flexibility and capability of material design.

4.7.2 Parametric Design of Porous Structures

Designing porous structures through the parametric optimization of cellular structures, particularly those utilizing triply periodic minimal surfaces (TPMS), represents a significant advancement in material science and structural engineering [3, 48,50–53]. TPMS, such as the gyroid, and diamond surfaces (as depicted in Figure 4.9), offer an advantage due to their periodicity in three independent spatial directions and their characteristic mean curvature of zero at every point on the surface. These surfaces, often used in the design of porous materials, provide a versatile framework for modeling various geometric configurations. Their intrinsic properties allow for the creation of highly complex and varied porous structures through the manipulation of parametric models [54].

A key benefit of using TPMS in porous structure design lies in their ability to be parametrically adjusted to achieve different geometries. For instance, a gyroid surface, a specific type of TPMS, is characterized by its periodic and minimal surface properties which make it particularly amenable to changes in structural parameters [55–57]. By adjusting these parameters, designers can precisely control the size and shape of the apertures within the porous material. This flexibility is illustrated in Figure 4.8, where different porosity gradient distributions are achieved by varying the parameters of the TPMS model.

The fundamental principle behind these adjustments involves the implicit function expressions that define the TPMS. The gyroid surface, for example, is described by an implicit function that encodes the periodic surface features. By altering parameters within this function, one can fine‐tune the pore sizes and distribution, allowing for the customization of the porous material's structural properties to meet specific engineering requirements.

4.7.3 Non‐Parametric Design of Porous Structures

4.7.3.1 Mono‐Scale

Mono‐scale topology optimization is a powerful approach in the design of porous structures. Unlike multi‐scale modeling, which relies on the assumption of distinct separation between different length scales, mono‐scale optimization operates under the principle that material distribution can be optimized within a single scale. This approach is grounded in the discretization of the design domain into a finite‐sized mesh, where each element of the mesh represents a portion of the material. As the mesh resolution increases, the resulting structures can exhibit intricate, fine‐scale features that are essential for optimizing stiffness and other mechanical properties.

The process of mono‐scale topology optimization involves the allocation of materials within the design domain to achieve optimal structural performance under specified loading conditions. By iteratively adjusting the material distribution, the optimization algorithm seeks to minimize or maximize an objective function, such as structural compliance, while adhering to constraints on material volume or other design parameters.

One of the key advantages of mono‐scale topology optimization is its ability to generate highly efficient structural designs without the need to delve into the complexity of multi‐scale modeling. As the mesh resolution becomes finer, the optimized structures can naturally evolve to exhibit multi‐scale characteristics. The successful application of mono‐scale topology optimization to the design of porous structures requires meticulous attention to several critical factors. Among these, the continuation technique employed during the optimization process plays a pivotal role. Continuation techniques involve the gradual variation of specific parameters within the optimization algorithm to guide the search toward a globally optimal solution. Without these strategies, the algorithm is prone to premature convergence to suboptimal solutions, particularly in complex design spaces characterized by numerous local optima. Equally important is the local control of material distribution within the design domain. Two primary strategies for achieving this control are pattern repetition and local volume constraints. In the pattern repetition strategy, the design domain is divided into multiple subdomains. Each subdomain undergoes refinement to allow for detailed material distribution, with the layout constrained to be identical across all subdomains. This approach ensures periodic patterns throughout the structure, making it particularly advantageous for applications requiring uniformity and regularity, such as biomedical implants and filtration devices.

For applications necessitating graded or varying material distributions, subdomains can be grouped, with each group sharing a common material layout. This method enables the creation of structures exhibiting periodic patterns along one axis while allowing material variation along another axis. Such flexibility is essential for designing structures that must perform optimally under varying load conditions or environmental influences. The implementation of these local control strategies often relies on templates that establish links between subdomains within the design domain. These templates serve as references for material layout, where the sensitivity of each template point is computed as the aggregate sensitivity of the corresponding points in all linked subdomains. This approach effectively reduces the optimization problem's solution space, as the algorithm only needs to determine the material layout for a single subdomain or a small group of subdomains. Despite this reduction, the optimization and analysis are conducted on the full mesh, ensuring that the final structure is optimized at the resolution of the entire design domain. However, the computational power needed is phenomenally expensive for topology optimization.

4.7.3.2 Multi‐scale Approach

Multi‐scale topology optimization for designing porous structures represents an advanced lightweight engineering structure, characterized by its ability to optimize material distribution across multiple scales, thereby achieving superior performance with low computational cost. This optimization process is inherently mathematically complex due to the need to manage interactions across different scales, from the macroscale down to the microscale, and vice versa where material behavior and structural performance must be simultaneously considered. The challenge lies in the appropriate representation and optimization of the unit cells, the fundamental building blocks of the structure, which dictate the overall physical properties through their geometric and material characteristics.

As the restrictions on unit cell design increase, the flexibility in the parameterization of these cells decreases. In highly restricted approaches, unit cells are parameterized by a single parameter, such as density, which significantly simplifies the optimization process but also limits the achievable structural performance. This trade‐off between design flexibility and computational efficiency is a key consideration in multi‐scale topology optimization. The optimization process in these cases typically involves predefined cellular structures, where the effective material properties of the unit cells are evaluated using homogenization techniques. Homogenization is a powerful tool in multi‐scale modeling, allowing for the computation of effective material properties by averaging the microscale behavior over a representative volume element. This process is computationally efficient because it can be performed offline, prior to the actual optimization, thereby reducing the computational burden during the design process.

The use of predefined unit cells in multi‐scale topology optimization offers several advantages, particularly in the context of additive manufacturing, where these cells can be directly translated into 3D‐printed components. The optimization of such structures often requires minimal adaptation of traditional mono‐scale optimization techniques, as the repeating unit cells can be treated as materials whose distribution is optimized across the structure. This approach is particularly attractive for designing components with uniform infill patterns, which are commonly used in industries such as aerospace and biomedical engineering. The simplicity of this method lies in its ability to reduce the solution space of the optimization problem, often to a single dimension, thereby facilitating the construction of differentiable functions that map the reduced parameters to the homogenized properties of the material.

However, the use of predefined unit cells also imposes certain limitations, particularly when dealing with anisotropic materials. Most mono‐scale optimization techniques are traditionally designed for isotropic materials, where the material properties are uniform in all directions. In contrast, anisotropic materials exhibit direction‐dependent properties, which require modifications to the stiffness matrices used in FEA. These modifications ensure that the homogenized elasticity tensor accurately reflects the anisotropic nature of the material, thereby preserving the integrity of the optimization results. The challenge is further compounded when the anisotropic microstructures are not aligned with the local principal stress directions, which can lead to suboptimal performance and structural inefficiencies.

To address these challenges, advanced multi‐scale topology optimization approaches have been developed that allow for greater flexibility in unit cell parameterization. These approaches include hierarchical and concurrent optimization methods, where the microstructures are represented by multi‐variable density fields. In hierarchical optimization, the structure is sequentially optimized at different scales, with the microscale optimization feeded into the macroscale design. This allows for the creation of structures that are optimized for both global and local performance criteria. Concurrent optimization, on the other hand, treats the structure as a unified entity, where the optimization is performed simultaneously across all scales. This approach is computationally more demanding but offers the potential for achieving truly optimal multi‐scale designs.

Mono‐scale topology optimization concentrates on optimizing the parameterized aspects of microstructures, such as material distribution, within a single, uniformly discretized scale, typically through a finely meshed design domain. While this approach allows for the emergence of fine‐scale features as the mesh resolution increases, it is inherently limited by the need to balance the trade‐offs between resolution and computational cost. As the mesh becomes finer, the computational resources required for optimization grow exponentially, often leading to prohibitive computational expenses, especially when addressing the complex interactions that occur at both the macro‐ and microscale levels in porous structures.

In contrast, multi‐scale topology optimization offers a more sophisticated approach by explicitly considering the separation of scales, thereby optimizing material distribution across multiple distinct scales simultaneously. This method significantly reduces computational cost while maintaining a high level of detail in the design of porous structures. By employing homogenization techniques and hierarchical modeling, multi‐scale optimization effectively decouples the macro‐ and microscale problems, allowing for the efficient computation of effective material properties at the microscale, which can then be utilized in the macroscale optimization. This not only reduces the dimensionality of the problem but also enables the design of structures with intricate microscale porosity embedded within a robust macroscale framework.

Moreover, the ability of multi‐scale optimization to craft highly detailed structures at both scales is particularly advantageous in applications requiring precise control over material properties, such as in biomedical implants or advanced filtration systems. The method's capacity to seamlessly integrate fine‐scale features into a global structure without overwhelming computational demands positions it as a superior choice for designing porous materials. While mono‐scale optimization may excel in generating detailed structures when computational resources are ample, multi‐scale topology optimization emerges as the better approach overall, particularly for large‐scale, complex designs where computational efficiency and the ability to address interactions across scales are paramount.

Chapter 5
Practical Examples and Case Studies

5.1 INTRODUCTION

This chapter presents a series of case studies focused on the design of porous structures for a range of applications. These include lightweight heat dissipators, multiphysics problems requiring high mechanical stiffness and low heat absorptivity, as well as the design of soft robotics utilizing single‐ and multi‐materials. Additionally, the chapter introduces the multiphysics principles of heat‐harvesting mechanisms and grippers. The chapter encompasses both mathematical modeling and practical case studies to demonstrate how the final design of porous structures depends on the initial design of both the micro‐ and macrostructures. Notably, the details of the multiphysics model for high mechanical stiffness and low heat absorptivity will be discussed further in Chapter 6.

5.2 POROUS HEAT SINK DESIGNS WITH NON‐PARAMETRIC OPTIMIZATION

Thermally conductive solid structures, such as passive thermal solid‐state components, are extensively integrated into industrial systems, particularly as the exigency for high‐frequency data transmission intensifies with the advent of technologies like 5G cellular communication and autonomous vehicles. Traditional designs of thermal dissipation components often occupy significant physical space and impose excessive weight on printed circuit boards (PCBs), potentially leading to long‐term degradation of the printed wiring. This degradation arises from mechanical stress, including bending due to the component's weight or from resonant vibrations interacting with cooling fins, which can also alter the high‐frequency impedance characteristics. Consequently, the development of a lightweight, efficient heat dissipater becomes a highly sought‐after solution to mitigate these challenges [1–8].

In the context of non‐parametric optimization for the thermal conductivity of lightweight structures, shape and topology optimization strategies often focus on minimizing thermal compliance. This approach aims to reduce stored thermal energy in favor of enhancing heat conduction efficiency. While thermal compliance has been effectively employed as an objective function in the design of high‐conductivity solid structures, achieving an optimal design necessitates meticulous consideration of factors such as the discretization of the design domain and the intricacies of the optimization process [9–12]. In terms of designing thermal solutions, heat compliance is a major thermal performance metric used in optimization problems, particularly for heat sink designs, representing the system's resistance to heat flow and thermal energy distribution. So, with high thermal compliance, the design tends to store heat energy, while with low thermal compliance, heat dissipation is maximized, making it ideal for heat sink applications.

In the realm of thermal compliance minimization, researchers initially reported tree‐like configurations as effective design solutions. However, when the finite element resolution within the design domain exceeds a million elements, the emergence of spike‐like geometries, as observed by Wadbro et al., [13] becomes prevalent. This shift underscores the complexity inherent in optimizing thermal structures. Yan et al. [14] provided an in‐depth exploration of the thermal compliance optimization challenge and the characteristics of tree‐like designs. Their study benchmarks various study cases, highlighting how the selection of design variable interpolation schemes, conditions, and the initial design domain can profoundly influence the achievement of optimal solutions. Notably, their findings challenge the conventional wisdom that tree‐like structures are optimal for maximizing thermal conductivity.

Building on this foundation, we propose that by concurrently optimizing both the micro‐ and macrostructures of a material, one can achieve a superior performance‐to‐weight ratio under stringent design constraints. Specifically, optimizing the material layout within the representative volume element (RVE) as a secondary design domain, nested within the macroscale, can significantly enhance the overall performance of the structure. This approach suggests that by refining the microstructural arrangement, materials can achieve an extremum in performance metrics, resulting in a markedly higher performance‐to‐weight ratio, thereby pushing the boundaries of current thermal optimization paradigms. In this part, we address a critical gap in the design methodology for achieving fin‐like structures, as opposed to conventional tree‐like thermal structures, through the application of concurrent multi‐scale topology optimization. The primary objective of this work is to develop and rigorously examine the methodologies and formulations necessary for effective concurrent multi‐scale optimization in the context of thermal conductivity. This involves the exploration of the interpolation method that links the macro‐ and microscales within the design system, thereby enabling the seamless integration of multi‐scale design elements. Moreover, this part introduces a predetermined initial design domain scheme, which is pivotal in the pursuit of a robust three‐dimensional (3D) printable porous structure with enhanced thermal conductivity.

5.2.1 Simultaneous Multi‐scale Formulation of Thermal Conductivity Optimization for Porous Heat Sink Using Topological Methods

The simultaneous optimization of macro‐ and microsystems was conducted through concurrent topology optimization, aiming to minimize heat compliance at both scales, i.e., the micro (xM)‐ and macroscale (xm). The macro‐ and microscale design domains were discretized using separate finite element systems, with bilinear structured meshes employed for both. In this framework, an elemental design variable (x) of 1 indicates solid material, while 0 represents a void, as expressed in Eq. (5.1).

(5.1)

The concurrent design of multi‐scale problems necessitates the adoption of homogenization methods, which serve two critical purposes. The first is the determination of effective material properties, which are essential for the accurate modeling and analysis of the macrostructure.

The second purpose involves the utilization of inverse homogenization as a sophisticated tool for the concurrent design of both microstructure and macrostructure. In this context, inverse homogenization facilitates the tailored design of the microstructure to achieve desired macroscopic properties, enabling the optimization process to address both scales simultaneously. This dual application of homogenization methods ensures that the microstructural design is not only compatible with, but also enhances, the overall performance of the macrostructure, thereby achieving an optimized multi‐scale design that meets stringent thermal conductivity requirements.

5.2.2 Homogenization Approach of Linking the Multi‐scale for Heat Conduction Problem

Homogenization has been employed numerically to assess the effective properties, encapsulated by the homogenized conductivity tensor ΛH, of the microstructural design domain. By definition, this micro design domain represents an RVE that is statistically homogeneous when compared to the larger macroscale domain. The effective properties derived from this homogenization process are influenced by several factors, including the material distribution within the RVE, the intrinsic behavior of the properties under consideration within the material, and the interfacial behavior at the boundaries between different material phases.

In the context of this study, it is assumed that thermal conductivity is uniformly distributed throughout the material. Additionally, the effects of the interface layer between different material phases have been neglected, simplifying the RVE to a single‐phase infinitesimal solid material interspersed with voids. This simplification allows for a more tractable numerical analysis while still capturing the essential characteristics of the material's thermal behavior.

By applying the asymptotic homogenization method, the effective properties of the microstructure are determined and subsequently utilized to construct the finite element model of the macroscale domain. Specifically, this approach considers a two‐phase microstructure, where the overall thermal conductivity is represented by Λk, k = 1, 2, denoting the thermal conductivity tensors associated with the respective phases of the RVE. The general steady‐state heat conduction within this system is governed by Eq. (5.2).

This formulation serves as the foundation for understanding and predicting the thermal behavior of the material system across multiple scales, thereby enabling the concurrent optimization of both microstructural and macrostructural properties.

5.2.4 Numerical Investigations of Concurrent Multi‐ Versus Macroscale Optimization

The numerical investigation presented in this section focuses on comparing macroscale optimization with multi‐scale optimization approaches. Initially, we consider a macro design domain consisting of 200 × 200 elements along the x and y directions, with a specified volume fraction of 0.5, corresponding to a 50% reduction in material weight. A uniformly distributed heat load of 0.1 is applied across the entire area, while a heat sink is positioned in the middle of the left boundary on the upper left side (as illustrated in Figure 5.1(a)). The remaining outer boundaries are assumed to be adiabatic, thus preventing heat transfer across them.

When optimizing for heat compliance at the macroscale level, the resulting design is depicted in Figure 5.2(a). This optimization, constrained by the initial 50% volume fraction, produces a structure that effectively balances material distribution with thermal performance. However, when the volume fraction is further reduced to 0.25, representing a 75% weight reduction, the final heat compliance significantly increases due to the excessive reduction in material, as shown in Figure 5.2(b). This outcome highlights the trade‐off between weight reduction and thermal efficiency when relying solely on macroscale optimization.

To address this challenge, we employ a concurrent optimization strategy that simultaneously optimizes both the macro‐ and microscales of the design domain. By integrating multi‐scale considerations, volume reduction is achieved across both scales, resulting in a design that maintains thermal performance despite significant material reduction. The microstructural optimization, illustrated in Figure 5.1(b), is destined to play an important role not only in weighing minimization but also in enhancing the overall thermal conductivity of the structure, compensating for the reduced material at the macroscale. This approach demonstrates the superior efficacy of multi‐scale optimization in achieving lightweight designs with optimized thermal properties, surpassing the limitations observed in single‐scale optimization.

Therefore, by assuming that each element in the design domain corresponds to a volume equivalent to that of the microscale, the multi‐scale optimization approach—achieving a 50% weight reduction at both the macro‐ and microscales—effectively results in a 75% total volume reduction. The results of this multi‐scale optimization, depicted in Figure 5.2(c), demonstrate superior performance in minimizing heat compliance compared to the equivalent 75% total volume reduction achieved solely through macroscale optimization.

5.2.5 Numerical Investigation of Concurrent Multi‐scale Optimization with Predetermined Initial Design Domain

By introducing predetermined branched design domains for the problem discussed in Section 5.2.4, we achieved a fin‐like structure in the final design (as illustrated in Figure 5.3). The performance of the design was significantly enhanced by optimizing the multi‐scale problem with an initial design domain that incorporated an increased number of branches. As demonstrated in Figure 5.3(b)–(d), this approach led to improved heat compliance minimization compared to the multi‐scale design problem presented in Section 5.2.4.

The introduction of initial branched structures allowed for more efficient heat dissipation pathways, resulting in a more effective thermal management solution. This improvement highlights the importance of initial design considerations in multi‐scale topology optimization for thermal problems [18, 19].

5.2.6 3D Concurrent Topology Optimization of Heat Dissipater

The 3D cases were optimized using the SIMP method, as illustrated in Figure 5.4. In these examples, the design domains are uniformly heated at all nodes. For case (a), a heat sink is positioned at the center of the upper surface, while in case (b), it is located at the upper corner, both maintained at a temperature of 0°. The weight reduction for both the micro‐ and macroscales is set to 50%, with a uniform heat load of 0.01 applied to the macroscale nodes.

The microscale design domain begins with an initial rectangular hole shape that extends through all surfaces. Structured hexahedral elements were employed for the finite element modeling of both the macro‐ and microscale domains. The microscale design domain utilized 8,000 hexahedral elements, while the macroscale domain used 144,000 elements. Table 5.1 presents the optimized topologies of the macro‐ and microstructures for the two cases depicted in Figure 5.4.

It is important to note that 3D finite element analysis demands significantly higher computational resources compared to simpler two‐dimensional (2D) models. Consequently, a lower mesh resolution was used, resulting in voxelized designs, as shown in the first two columns of Table 5.1. Increasing the number of elements can enhance the precision and sharpness of the design but comes with a significant computational cost. To address this challenge and improve the visualization of 3D designs while maintaining low mesh resolution, we employed a volume imaging approach using the Isosurface technique [21]. This method enables the creation of high‐quality design representations, as shown in the third and fourth columns of Table 5.1. The Isosurface technique effectively generates detailed images from voxelized data with minimal computational requirements, ensuring both accuracy and efficiency in representing spatial configurations [22, 23]. This technique is widely used in medical imaging [24] and fluid dynamics [25]. The objective history of the case (a) is presented in Figure 5.5.

To demonstrate the practicality of using Isosurface rendering for creating robust 3D‐printed models, we opted for a smaller and simpler model to optimize and manufacture. Cases (a) and (b) involved relatively large numbers of elements, which would significantly increase in size when meshed microstructures are introduced for each macrostructural element. Therefore, we selected a smaller problem, with 150 elements in the macroscale domain, where heat loading is applied to the lower side and a heat sink is placed at the central part of the upper side (as depicted in Figure 5.4(c)).

The multi‐scale optimization was performed using a microstructure design domain similar to those in cases (a) and (b). The resulting designs for both the macro‐ and microscales are presented in Table 5.1, case (c). Using Isosurface rendering, the 3D model of the multi‐scale design was converted into STereoLithography (STL) format for 3D printing (as illustrated in Figure 5.6).

To validate the feasibility of manufacturing the refined model, we fabricated the design illustrated in Figure 5.6. Acrylonitrile butadiene styrene (ABS) was utilized as the material. The preparation and printing of the model were carried out using a Stratasys F170 3D printer, as shown in Figure 5.7.

5.2.7 Numerical Investigation of the Effect of Microstructure Dependency on Multi‐scale Design

This section explores the complex relationship between macroscopic and microscopic design domains and their influence on initial microstructure through a concurrent multi‐scale optimization framework. Utilizing topology optimization with the SIMP method, we aimed to reduce the solid design domain's weight by 25% through equal 50% reductions in both solid structure and microstructure weights. The numerical model, depicted in Figure 5.7, features a 150 mm × 150 mm macro design domain discretized into 1 mm2 elements, with an initial design value of XM = 0.5 to meet the SIMP criteria. The micro design domain, measuring 1 mm × 1 mm, employs periodic boundary conditions for numerical homogenization and is discretized into 104 elements for effective microscale analysis and optimization. This approach allows for a comprehensive investigation of the intricate interplay between scales in the optimization process. Three distinct examples of the initial micro design domain have been investigated. The first example within our study introduces a central circular sub‐design domain Ω2 characterized by an initial value of 0.5, while the remainder of the micro design domain Ω1 maintains a consistent initial value of 1.

This variation in the central sub‐design domain, as opposed to the surrounding areas, serves multiple scientific purposes. Primarily, this differentiation is employed to encourage achieving optimal design outcomes through the optimality criteria, all while significantly reducing computational cost [26]. Additionally, it aids in the minimization of grayscale regions within the microcell [20, 27]. The investigation expands to include two additional microscale configurations, each featuring distinct central subdomains. The second system incorporates a square subdomain, while the third employs a subdomain of unspecified shape, both with side lengths of 50 elements. These designs are purposefully selected to examine how different geometric forms affect microstructural optimization. Importantly, the initial values assigned to these subdomains are kept consistent with those used in the previously studied circular subdomain case.

This approach, exploring various subdomain shapes while maintaining consistent initial conditions, enables a comprehensive analysis of how both geometry and starting parameters influence the optimization process. The study aimed to shed light on the intricate interplay between subdomain configuration and initial settings in shaping the outcomes of microstructural optimization.

We have selected initial design values of Ω1 = 1 and Ω2 = 0.5 for the purpose of conducting multi‐scale optimization across three distinct initial microstructures, as depicted in Figure 5.8(a)–(d). The analysis, summarized in Table 5.2, reveals varying outcomes across different microstructures in multi‐scale topology optimization for heat compliance reduction. The square sub‐design domain proved to be the least effective in this regard. In contrast, the circular sub‐design domain demonstrated a notable 22% enhancement in performance. The most promising results were obtained with the rhombus sub‐design domain, which achieved a remarkable 25% decrease in heat compliance compared to the square configuration. This significant improvement, illustrated in Figure 5.9, underscores the rhombus domain's superior efficacy in this particular optimization scenario. These findings highlight the crucial role that sub‐design domain geometry plays in determining the effectiveness of multi‐scale topology optimization for heat compliance minimization.

The data in Table offer insights into how the initial microscale central sub‐design domain affects concurrent multi‐scale optimization, especially regarding the enhancement of heat conductivity. To gain an in‐depth understanding of the role of subdomains at the microscale in the optimization process, we conducted a detailed analysis using a rhombus‐shaped central sub‐design domain as a case study. This investigation involved varying the parameters of the sub‐design domain and categorizing the resulting configurations into four distinct groups. The first group consists of a central rhombus‐shaped sub‐design domain, as depicted in Figure 5.8(d), with the corresponding results summarized in Table 5.3.

5.2.8 Multiphysics Optimization Aimed at Maximizing Heat Conductivity and Mechanical Stiffness

In real‐world applications, the design problem must satisfy multiple physical aspects. These problems are referred to as multiphysics problems. The constituent physical aspects have their own formulations and necessitate distinct discretization to satisfy these formulations and associated conditions. The involved physics may exhibit complementary relationships or may be distinct and separate. In this section, the multiphysics problem of designing a high‐stiffness heat dissipator is addressed.

In order to maximize heat conductivity, heat compliance CH is minimized. Using the same analogy, mechanical stiffness maximization is achieved by minimizing mechanical compliance Cmech. The generalized form of the main mechanical objective function takes the form of Eq. (5.22):

(5.22)

where v represent the volume fraction constraints of the macro‐()M and microsystem ()m. As such, transforming the objective function to the normalized form is adopted in this work, and it takes the form of

(5.23)

where fc* and fκ* are, respectively, the function at the attainable design for the compliance and thermal conductivity problems, which is calculated by optimizing each case separately and evaluating each objective function to the optimal design. fcini and fκini are, respectively, the compliance of the initial topology and the thermal conductivity of the microstructure. w1 and w2 are the weighing factors such that w1 + w2 = 1. In terms of normalized multi‐objective functions that are used as the target of multi‐scale and multiphysics topology optimization in this work, it takes the form of

(5.24)

Topology optimization requires sensitivity analysis capable of addressing the multiphysics aspects of the problem to guide the search algorithm toward identifying the extremum point. For the function presented in Eq. (5.24), given that the denominator remains constant with respect to the objective function, the sensitivity of the function can be expressed as

The derivative of the homogenized material's thermal conductivity tensor with respect to the micro design variable has also been derived previously in Eq. (5.18).

The optimization process can be summarized in the following steps:

  1. Step 1: Specify the design domain parameters for both the macro‐ and microscale. The input parameters include the discretization resolution (number of elements along the x and y axes), the desired volume fraction, and the material properties of the base materials used for developing the microstructure design in conjunction with the macrostructure design.
  2. Step 2: The design domain and finite element analysis are structured to ensure the creation of a specialized multi‐scale finite element model tailored to each physical phenomenon within the domain. In this approach, a single physical model treats every element of the macro‐scale design domain as a unit cell, which is further discretized using a microscale framework. By employing the homogenization method, the effective macroscopic properties of the RVE are determined, accounting for the distinct and well‐defined phases of the materials at the microscale [28]. In this context, robustness refers to ensuring the ergodicity of the mechanical properties of the selected RVE for the macrostructure. If the RVE size is too small, it may exclude certain material phases from consideration, compromising the accuracy of the representation [29]. Therefore, the size of the RVE relative to its sub‐constructive phases must be sufficiently large to guarantee numerical stability when applying periodic boundary conditions and to accurately represent the effective properties of the selected macrostructure [30].
  3. Step 3: Calculate the initial design values for mechanical and thermal compliances (fcini and fκini, respectively) is essential as a preliminary step in constructing the scalarized form of the normalized multi‐objective function.
  4. Step 4: Calculate the optimized results for each physical model to identify the values of fc* and fκ*.
  5. Step 5: Calculate the objective function of the macro‐ and microscale (Eq. 5.24). Because the summation of the weights is 1, the weighing can be written in terms of a general weight (η). As such, the weights are rewritten as w1 = η and w2 = (1 − η).
  6. Step 6: Perform the sensitivity analysis and evaluate the design domains with the optimality criteria, and update the design domain.
  7. Step 7: Assess the design variables with respect to the constraint functions to ensure compliance with the weight minimization criteria for both the macro‐ and micro design variables.
  8. Step 8: Establish the design domain by assigning the macro design variables xM as 1 for solid elements and associate it with micro design variables xm that have the value of 1 for solids and 0 for the voids of the microscale design domain. As a result, the layout of the macrostructure and the microstructure is updated simultaneously. This will construe into a porous macrostructure with the highest possible heat conductivity and high stiffness with high weight reduction.
  9. Step 9: Iterate through steps 5–8 until the structural layout meets the specified constraints.

The overall algorithm for concurrent multi‐scale and multiphysics topology optimization, aimed at maximizing heat conductivity and mechanical stiffness, is depicted in Figure 5.10.

The first model features a rectangular domain comprising 200 elements along the x‐axis and 90 elements along the y‐axis. It incorporates a periodic square microstructure with an edge length of 100 elements. Both share a volume fraction reduction of 50%. In the mechanical model, the left edge is fixed, and a downward load is applied at a point located in the third quarter of the right edge, measured from the top (refer to Figure 5.11(a)). For the heat conduction model, the temperature at the center of the left edge is set to zero (Figure 5.11(b)), while all other boundaries are treated as adiabatic, ensuring no heat transfer, following the Bejan method [19, 32, 33]. Heat generation was set to be constant and homogeneous throughout the domain of 0.01, which is represented in a finite element, another discretization with the same size as for the mechanical model (i.e., with 200 × 90 elements in the x and y directions, respectively, for macroscale and 100 × 100 elements for the microscale with periodic boundary conditions to comply with the numerical homogenization evaluation scheme). Regarding the initial micro design domain, it is set to have the value of 0.5 except the central circular domain of the radius equal to 50 elements. This central sub‐design domain was set to have a quarter value of the initial value than for the other design domains. This is to encourage achieving a design by the optimality criteria with less computational cost [26] as well as minimizing the grayscale area of the microcell [27]. The optimization results for the concurrent multi‐scale dimension are shown in Figure 5.12. The influence of the trade‐off of the multi‐objective multi‐scale problem is shown in the change of the macro‐ and microstructures. The microstructure is trying to adapt to the stronger physics while balancing the micro design variables with the overall objective of decreasing the value of the general weighing factor η. The Pareto front shows that the best result (knee point) for the first example is for the weighing factor (η = 0.6). The history of the multi‐objective function is shown in Figures 5.12 and 5.13.

The second example's mechanical model (Figure 5.11(c)) features a square macro design domain with a side length of 150 elements. The left side is fixed, and a load is applied at the center of the right edge, directed along the positive y‐axis. The solid heat conduction model retains properties similar to the first example but differs in the placement of the heat sink (Figure 5.11(d)).

The Pareto front for macro‐ and microscale optimization is illustrated in Figure 5.14. The transition in macrostructure layouts between the two cases is notably reflected in the microscale design, particularly in case 2. This transition directly corresponds to the combined effects of the physical models. Material distribution changes in response to the boundary conditions of each physical model. In the first example (Figure 5.11(a) and (b)), the boundary conditions guide the macro design to extend toward the middle of the left side, except in the mechanical model, where it shifts slightly downward. The microscale design adapts by distributing material diagonally for the heat conduction model, with a concentrated mass at the center to facilitate thermal energy transfer within the microstructure.

For the mechanical strain energy model, the microscale layout distributes energy along the edges and diagonally, forming a branching pattern. This includes a triangular truss arrangement around the central circular void of the microstructure. Table 5.4 summarizes the optimal results of the multi‐scale optimization for both examples.

5.3 APPLICATION OF ROBOTICS

Robotics are increasingly employed in a wide range of applications, encompassing various forms and categories. These include welding robots in automotive manufacturing plants, automated domestic vacuum cleaners, and even assistive systems in modern vehicles, such as steering assistance and auto‐parking technologies.

Robotics leverages compliant mechanisms to achieve high precision and reliability by utilizing elastic deformation for motion and force transmission. Unlike traditional mechanisms with joints and bearings, compliant mechanisms minimize friction, backlash, and wear, enabling smooth and repeatable movements. This makes them ideal for applications requiring micrometer‐level accuracy, such as surgical robotics, precision assembly, and soft robotics. By integrating flexibility and structural efficiency, compliant mechanisms enhance the performance and versatility of robotic systems, driving innovation in high‐precision tasks.

A common necessity for all robotic systems is minimizing energy consumption, which can be achieved through several approaches. One of the primary strategies is weight reduction. Designing porous robotic structures facilitates the creation of lightweight robots, significantly contributing to energy efficiency. Furthermore, advancements in 3D‐printing technology enable the efficient and precise fabrication of porous robotic structures, making this approach accessible for both enthusiasts and industrial‐scale manufacturing. This section introduces several applications aimed at achieving porous robotics. These applications are discussed in detail, with a focus on optimization and simulation techniques.

5.3.1 Porous Soft Robotic Design with Hybrid Topology Optimization

Soft robotics has been the subject of strong interest in the last few years, both from researchers and industry. Roboticist is considered a potentially whole new class of machines that perform better in the real world and are more versatile [34–37]. Soft robotics aims to develop robots that are flexible and compliant, mimicking the properties of biological organisms. Similar to natural tissues, the primary structure of a soft robot distributes energy to perform actions, deforming in response to external forces rather than relying solely on rigid linkages and gears as in conventional robotics. In essence, soft robotics seeks to extend the field beyond the constraints of dynamic rigid bodies.

This study focuses on designing an ultra‐lightweight, stiff, soft robotic gripping hand capable of achieving precise gripping with a single actuation point. The approach involves two key strategies: first, incorporating a structural design that redistributes strain energy to achieve controlled, precise displacements; second, employing a porous structure to minimize weight while maintaining superior performance. Additionally, the pores are optimized to maximize material properties, such as resistance to axial and shear stresses.

To achieve this design, the study employs non‐parametric optimization, specifically topology optimization, which is one of the most effective methods for generating optimal designs that meet user‐defined objectives. This technique involves discretizing the design space and applying conventional optimization algorithms to determine the optimal topology of the structure. For static loading scenarios, the optimization process requires defining load steps, design variables, responses, constraints, and objectives to solve the problem effectively [4, 5, 7, 8]. Associated with additive manufacturing, topologically optimized structures have shown robust design and good performance [38–40]. Topology optimization has revolutionized the design of lightweight and highly functional structures. By optimizing the distribution of materials within a given design domain, this approach maximizes the structural performance‐to‐weight ratio while meeting various design constraints. One of the pioneering methods in continuum topology optimization for creating compliant systems is the homogenization approach. This technique simplifies complex structural optimization problems by reformulating them as efficient multi‐scale optimization tasks, using a material density function for each discretized element. These elements are conceptualized as containing an infinite number of randomly distributed voids.

Homogenization theory plays a critical role in determining the mechanical properties of such materials. There are two primary strategies for incorporating microstructures: rank laminate composites and hollow microcell‐based designs. In the case of rank laminate composites, the homogenization equations can often be solved analytically. Conversely, for hollow microcells, numerical methods are typically employed to solve the homogenization problem. With the homogenization method, theoretical structural performance can be mathematically constrained [41]. Ananthasuresh et al [42] extended the homogenization to perform compliant mechanism designs. However, because the resulting mechanisms are not flexible enough, the results appear to be a mean compliance design rather than a compliant mechanism design. As a result, Nishiwaki et al. [43] developed a homogenization‐based topology optimization method for the design of compliant mechanisms that includes flexibility. In their method and in order to effectively describe flexibility, the creation of a multi‐objective function using mutual mean compliance was used. The SIMP method has been used to design compliant mechanisms as a direct descendant of the homogenization method [44–47]. Additionally, the evolutionary structural optimization (ESO) method was developed on the straightforward tenet of gradually removing wasteful material from a structure in order to get the optimum structure conceivable. The fundamental tenet of ESO is the elimination of the so‐called inefficient material directly from the building in order to create the best design, and it was first introduced by Min and Steven [48]. The cost function sensitivity is used to update the decision variables [20, 49]. Updates are based on the element sensitivity number that is generated by differentiating the objective function so that the elemental sensitivity and zero are identical for solid and soft elements, respectively. And as for the SIMP method, the ESO method was investigated for designing compliant mechanisms [50–53]. Due to the challenges in creating robust designs, the multi‐scale compliant mechanism has received little attention from researchers. In addition, due to the fluctuating effective properties of the grayscale elements at the start of the optimization process, the grayscale nature of such a problem, when it is optimized using the SIMP method, significantly restricts attaining extrema. The research of Sivapuram et al. [54] suggested an improved compliant mechanism optimization using the level‐set method. The binary element idea in the zero‐level‐set method was shown to be effective for robust soft‐compliant mechanism design.

In order to simultaneously optimize the objective function on both the macro‐xM and microscales xm, concurrent multi‐scale topology optimization was carried out. Two distinct finite element systems are used to discretize the macro‐ and microscale design domains. For both systems in this paper, we used a bilinear structured mesh. The sensitivity analysis is given in Eq. 5.44, taking into account that the mechanical loading vector F is design‐independent in our linear analysis.

(5.46)

In this work, the macrostructure is optimized using the SIMP approach, while the microstructure is optimized with the ESO method. This hybrid optimization approach enables the generation of robust and clear design concepts, significantly reducing computational costs. The OCM is also employed to update the design variables [5, 62]. To ensure the existence of solutions to the topology optimization problem and to prevent the emergence of checkerboard patterns, a sensitivity filter is applied.

5.3.1.3 Numerical Examples and Discussion

In this section, we will investigate several examples of soft grippers. The first model has 80 and 80 mm in the x and y directions (as shown in Figure 5.16 (a)). The microstructure has 100 × 100 elements in the x and y directions. The volume fraction condition for the microscale is 0.3 and 0.4 for the macroscale. For all cases, the elastic tensor for solid material (E0) is given in Eq (5.47):

(5.47)

The objective is maximizing the bulk modulus [63] of the microscale ( for 2D case). The microscale design is shown in Figure 5.16(b) and (c). The macroscale design is shown in Figure 5.16(d).

To assess the computational cost of the proposed hybrid (SIMP‐ESO) topology optimization method in comparison to the SIMP method, we performed multi‐scale concurrent SIMP topology optimization for the same given example (i.e., Figure 5.16(d)). The number of iterations for both the SIMP and hybrid (SIMP‐ESO) methods is presented in Figure 5.17. The results indicate that the hybrid method achieves a robust design in a shorter time, with the hybrid topology optimization method being nearly three times faster than the SIMP method.

The output displacement (Uout) of our hybrid multi‐scale concurrent topology optimization (as shown in Figure 5.18(a)), also showed better results (1.2 times better) than using the SIMP method alone (as shown in Figure 5.18(b)).

It is important to note that Bendsoe and Sigmund [64], after conducting a series of investigations with discrete optimality criteria optimization (DOCO) and the SIMP method, concluded that modeling‐compliant mechanisms using linear analysis—the approach used in this research for all cases—often results in poor or ineffective designs. They further emphasized that, in the best‐case scenario, linear analysis provides erroneous results, while in the worst‐case scenario, the outcomes are completely unsuitable for designing a compliant mechanism. As noted by Bendsoe and Sigmund, designing compliant mechanisms using topology optimization requires the use of geometrically non‐linear finite element modeling [64]. The numerical examples presented by Bendsoe and Sigmund were developed with a simple gradient descent algorithm in mind, specifically the OCM. One limitation of the OCM is its tendency to converge to local extrema, which necessitates careful selection of the gradient descent parameters to avoid this issue [14, 20]. Later, several researchers made use of the method of moving asymptotes (MMAs) [65, 66]. This is a versatile algorithm capable of handling various types of optimization problems. It relies on a convex approximation suitable for topology optimization; however, the effectiveness of the method is significantly influenced by the asymptote and move limitations [67]. Due to their inherent characteristics, the OCM and MMA methods are single‐point search algorithms, making them prone to falling into local minima [7], particularly as the penalization power of the SIMP method increases. These limitations highlight the need for further exploration of the role of non‐linear analysis. Through extensive investigations, our research confirms the first part of Bendsoe and Sigmund's statement, showing that linearized modeling aimed at maximizing displacement as an objective function for designing compliant mechanisms can lead to questionable results. Additionally, prior studies have successfully applied non‐linear finite element modeling to the design of a piezoelectric gripper [7]. This supports the second part of the statement by Bendsoe and Sigmund. However, in this study, we demonstrate a robust design with well‐formed hinges (Figure 5.18(a)) in the case of hybrid topology optimization. From the results, we conclude that the issue of not achieving optimal hinge formation in compliant mechanism design is not due to linear analysis. Furthermore, we extend our observation to non‐linear analysis, where the consideration of non‐linearity mitigates the significant changes in design variables caused by penalization, and the mesh‐independent filters are less severe. The Young's modulus of elasticity for the SIMP method is a third‐order variation, which influences in this case, badly influencing a higher nodal displacement value within the context of linear elastic finite element analysis, particularly when using bilinear elements. In this approach, rigid elements (where the design variables are set to unity) tend to concentrate around the hinges, which are expected to be present. However, in non‐linear analysis, iterative methods are highly sensitive to coupled analysis, causing small fields (due to the low values of penalized design variables) to become more pronounced. In contrast, our hybrid multi‐scale topology optimization begins with high values for the elastic tensors, which are then gradually reduced across all elements. This approach places the macro design variables in a situation akin to the modified rational approximation of material properties (RAMP) optimization. As a result, the solution avoids falling into local minima. Based on these findings, we conclude that the primary issue with the SIMP method in displacement control problems lies within the method itself, rather than the objective or finite element analysis. However, the SIMP method can yield excellent results when combined with problem‐specific modifications and parameterizations, such as using ESO to concurrently design the microscale in this section.

5.3.2 Multi‐material Porous Soft Robotics

Multi‐material structures represent an advanced engineering paradigm that integrates multiple materials within a single system to exploit their complementary properties, thereby optimizing the functionality of the structure. This methodology reflects the philosophy of strategically positioning materials within the spatial configuration of a structure to achieve superior performance metrics that cannot be realized with a single material alone.

While the fundamental philosophy of multi‐material structures aligns with that of composite materials—leveraging multiple physical materials to benefit from diverse properties—there is a key distinction. Rather than creating a "new material," multi‐material structures utilize the distinct physical responses of different materials in specific regions of the system to collectively achieve the desired structural performance.

Historically, the implementation of multi‐material structures was limited by high costs, despite their potential utility in applications such as suspension bridges (e.g., steel cables supporting concrete slabs and steel girders) and rocket engines. However, with advancements in 3D printing technologies, it has become feasible to fabricate structures comprising multiple materials with distinct physical properties. This innovation significantly simplifies the integration process, enabling the realization of optimized performance with greater ease.

In robotics, a critical requirement is minimizing energy consumption, which is often addressed through weight reduction. Designing porous robotic structures further enhances this objective by creating lightweight systems that improve energy efficiency. This section explores and discusses the design of multi‐material porous soft robotics, supported by numerical examples to illustrate their optimization and performance.

5.3.2.1 Mathematical Formulation for Hybrid Topology Optimization in Concurrent Multi‐material and Multi‐scale Systems

This section addresses the complexities of multi‐material systems by decomposing the problem into a series of individual single‐material sub‐problems. This strategy enables the development of a generalized SIMP interpolation model specifically designed for optimizing the topology of multi‐material‐compliant mechanisms. For instance, the interpolation model for the elastic modulus in a three‐phase material system can be expressed as follows:

ΩdM and Ωdm are the macro and micro design domains, respectively. The initial model used in this study had dimensions of 80 mm along the x‐axis and 160 mm along the y‐axis, as illustrated in Figure 5.19. The microstructure was represented using a grid of 100 × 100 elements in both the x and y directions. Volume fraction conditions were set at 0.5 for all microscale materials and 0.5 for the macroscale, leading to an overall weight reduction of 75% (corresponding to a 0.25 volume fraction). For all scenarios analyzed, the elastic tensors of the solid materials were consistent: the red material had a Young's modulus of 3, the green material had a Young's modulus of 2, and the blue material had a Young's modulus of 1. The case study revolves around the objective of maximization of the bulk modulus (denoted as ) within the aforementioned problem framework. 5.3.3 Heat‐Harnessing Porous Robotic Systems

In the previous section, the design of porous robotics was introduced. The activation of such robotic systems relies on the application of external forces at specific, predetermined points. These forces are typically generated through external actuation mechanisms, such as electric actuators, which derive their energy from an electrical power source. This energy is often produced via thermal energy conversion processes, such as those used in thermal power plants. However, energy conversion processes inherently involve losses, making it desirable to minimize power losses by directly harvesting activation energy.

In this section, a methodology for designing heat‐harvesting porous robotic systems is introduced, accompanied by multiple examples. Additionally, the additive manufacturability of these systems is addressed, highlighting their feasibility and potential for efficient production.

5.3.3.1 Mathematical Modeling of Thermo‐elastic Loading of Multiphysics‐Compliant Mechanism

Concurrent multi‐scale topology optimization is performed by establishing two independent representations of the design problem, i.e., macro‐ and microscale domains. The design variables related to discretization for topology optimization using two separate finite element systems are xM and xm, respectively, for macro‐ and microscale design domains. The macroscale domain is statistically homogeneous with respect to the microscale structure, which is regarded as the RVE. The effective characteristics of the microstructure are estimated using the numerical homogenization approach. In this study, the elastic tensor EH and the effective conductivity tensor ΛH are employed as the base material constant tensors for the macroscale. The thermoelastic loading formulations, homogenization theory, and the concurrent multi‐scale optimization of the multiphysics‐compliant mechanism will be discussed in the following subsections.

5.3.3.1.1 Thermoelastic Problem Formulation

The phenomenon of thermal deformation is the property of a substance to change the volume with temperature gradient within the structure. It's measured in terms of the coefficient of thermal expansion (αij). When a temperature gradient (ΔT) exists, it causes thermal loads in the continuum structures due to thermal expansion. The linear thermal strain (εth) caused by thermal deformation can be expressed by

(5.50)

In the analysis of material properties, the RVE framework employs a set of simplifying principles and analytical techniques. This model assumes a linear relationship in heat transfer and adopts small strain theory for elastic tensors, maintaining the analysis within the linear elastic domain. Numerical homogenization is achieved through finite element methods, with periodic boundary conditions applied to the RVE edges. The effective material characteristics are then determined by averaging field values. To ensure periodicity, the finite element model links opposite boundaries with equivalent field functions, interpolating across discretized segments. This methodology facilitates a thorough yet computationally manageable examination of intricate material attributes and responses. The direct coupling method presents significant challenges, particularly due to the complexity involved in achieving the multi‐scale sensitivity analysis required for topology optimization. Alternatively, the sequence coupling method provides computational benefits by utilizing specialized solvers tailored to individual fields. Each field is analyzed using numerical techniques and solvers best suited to its specific properties, simplifying implementation and reducing computational costs. This approach is especially advantageous for complex systems involving multiple interacting fields.

The decision to use sequence coupling or direct coupling depends on several factors, such as the complexity of the coupled system, the desired accuracy, and available computational resources. It is essential to balance accuracy and computational efficiency when selecting the most suitable coupling method. In this study, the sequence coupling method was adopted to address the thermo‐elastic coupling problem. Structural analysis of the thermal response was treated as quasi‐static, with the interaction of thermal and elastic fields represented by the superposition of displacements in the finite element framework, as shown in Eq. (5.51). This method enables efficient and accurate analysis of thermo‐elastic coupling while maintaining manageable computational costs.

(5.51)

where K is the global elastic stiffness matrix, U is the global nodal displacement, F is the elastic field nodal force, and Fth represents the thermally induced nodal forces. Fth is induced by the deformation in the structure due to temperature difference and takes the form of

(5.52)

where B is the strain displacement matrix, and E is the elastic tensor of the material. Here, the thermal gradient is calculated from a dedicated finite element model to describe the phenomena of heat transfer in solids.

5.3.3.1.2 Effective Elastic and Thermal Conductivity Tensors for the Microscale

By definition, the microscale structure is the RVE that is statistically homogeneous with respect to the macroscale domain. The effective characteristics of the microstructure are estimated using the numerical homogenization approach. In this work, the effective elastic tensor EH and the effective heat conductivity tensor ΛH are chosen as the basic material constant tensors at the macroscale. Additionally, with regard to the periodic micro design domain, the related thermal expansion effective property αH is also calculated. In this study, the interface layer between the material and the void is ignored, and the heat conductivity is assumed to be evenly distributed throughout the material. The RVE model incorporates several simplifying assumptions and methodologies. It presumes linear heat transmission and applies small deformation theory to elastic tensors, situating the structure within the ideal elastic range. The model employs numerical homogenization through finite element analysis, utilizing periodic boundary conditions on the RVE borders and deriving effective characteristics from average field calculations. To satisfy these periodic conditions, the cell finite element model connects opposing edge borders using identical field functions, which interpolate the field based on discretization segments. This approach enables a comprehensive yet computationally efficient analysis of complex material properties and behaviors [68]. The following part provides a succinct explanation and the basic equation for multi‐scale topology optimization.

The link between the effective properties and the local strain generated by thermal expansion is obtained via asymptotic expansion of the thermoelastic equation using perturbation theory. The elastic structure demonstrates compliant behavior by producing a displacement, represented as (uout), at a designated output port [72]. The goal of this optimization problem is to identify the optimal topology of the elastic structure that maximizes the output displacement while ensuring compliance with the equilibrium equations and adhering to a specified material volume constraint.
