To provide a summary, the concurrent model comprises a macrostructure with predefined boundary conditions, as depicted in Figure 5.25(a) and (b). The design domain ΩM consists of periodically replicated RVEs, with a designated design domain Ωm (as presented in Figure 5.25 (c)).

Each system, namely the macro‐ and microscale, is discretized using dedicated finite elements that correspond to the design variables.

5.3.3.3 Numerical Implementations

This section presents a range of case studies exploring the multiphysics multi‐scale thermoelastic formulation through various numerical examples. The investigations are structured into two main categories, each focusing on different aspects of the optimization process.

The first category examines the design of a reciprocal displacement actuator. We outline the optimization strategies employed and provide a comprehensive analysis of the outcomes achieved. This investigation sheds light on the intricacies of designing such actuators and the effectiveness of our optimization approach.

The second category focuses on a heat‐activated clipper mechanism. We detail the optimization techniques applied to this mechanism and offer an in‐depth discussion of the results and their implications. This study provides valuable insights into the design considerations and potential applications of heat‐activated clippers.

Through these detailed examinations, we aim to enhance our understanding of both the reciprocal displacement actuator and the heat‐activated clipper mechanism, contributing to the broader field of thermoelastic design optimization.

5.3.3.3.1 Reciprocal Heat‐Activated Displacement Actuator Optimization

To begin with, we'll explore the development of displacement actuators that respond to heat, considering designs both with and without heat convection at single and multiple scales. These actuators are engineered to generate linear movement by converting thermal energy into physical displacement.

Our initial focus was on the multiphysics topology optimization of a displacement actuator under thermal loading, excluding convection effects. We constructed a 2D macro design domain at a single scale, featuring insulation along the upper and lower boundaries (illustrated in Figure 5.26(a) and (c), respectively). This domain utilized bilinear finite element discretization (shown in Figure 5.26(b)) for both elastic and solid heat transfer physics simulations.

In the numerical simulations conducted in this study, material properties such as Young's modulus, thermal expansion coefficient, and heat convection tensor are assumed to have a unit value. This simplification enables a focused analysis of the specific phenomenon under investigation while also streamlining computational processes. During the topology optimization, the properties of void elements are assigned infinitesimal values rather than zero to minimize numerical errors and ensure stability throughout the optimization process [20, 74, 75]. The mesh for each model consists of 160 × 160 bilinear elements distributed along the x and y directions, with each element representing an area of 1 mm2. The optimization was initially conducted at a single scale, focusing solely on the macroscale. Two volume fractions were considered: (a) a volume fraction of 0.5, corresponding to a 50% weight reduction, and (b) a volume fraction of 0.25, corresponding to a 75% weight reduction. The results of the macroscale topology optimization, along with the objective function histories, are presented in Figure 5.27. Additionally, Figure 5.28 illustrates the deformation and heat map of the design that achieved the maximum deformation, specifically the design corresponding to the 75% weight reduction.

It was necessary to formulate the microstructure using a specific finite element model in order to extend the inquiry to encompass multi‐scale optimization. Periodic boundary conditions have been taken into account in order to address the microstructure in accordance with the numerical homogenization theory [21]. Consequently, a 100 × 100 element discretization of a finite element mesh was chosen at the microscale. The homogenization process and microstructure sensitivity analysis demand a substantial amount of processing power due to the large systems of equations that describe the behavior of the material at the microscale, which this mesh discretization allowed for a sufficient resolution of the microstructure while maintaining computational efficiency (Figure 5.29(a) and (b)).

The original design domain for the microstructure consists of a circular core subdomain with a radius equivalent to 50 elements. This central subdomain was initialized with a significantly smaller value compared to the remainder of the microstructure's design domain. This particular configuration was selected for two main purposes: first, to facilitate the optimization process using the optimality criteria while reducing computational expense [26]; second, to minimize numerical instability arising from cumulative errors [27, 75].

The multi‐scale optimization targeting a 50% weight reduction at both the macro‐ and microscales is designed to achieve a total volume fraction of 0.25 (equivalent to a 75% weight reduction), assuming that each element has a volume equal to that of the microscale. Consequently, significant volume reduction can be realized by minimizing weight at both the micro‐ and macrolevels. The results for the optimization of both the macro‐ and microscales are presented in Figure 5.30.

The performance of the optimized single‐ and multi‐scale cases is presented in Figure 5.31. From a physical perspective, designs with thicker membranes exhibit greater resistance to movement as compliant mechanisms due to their increased stiffness (i.e., reduced compliance), while designs with thinner linkages can achieve greater displacement for the same actuation field. This behavior is evident in the comparison of maximum displacement achieved for the 50% weight reduction versus the 75% weight reduction cases.

Furthermore, the concurrently optimized multi‐scale porous displacement actuator with a 75% weight reduction demonstrated superior performance compared to the single‐scale case with the same weight reduction, showing a 17.6% improvement over the 50% weight reduction case and a 6% improvement over the single‐scale 75% weight reduction case. The flexibility afforded by the microscale design domain allows the concurrent optimization algorithm to more effectively distribute materials for the porous heat‐activated displacement actuator, optimizing both the macro layout and the actuator's pose.

Let's examine the impact of heat convection on a single‐scale design domain, similar to that depicted in Figure 5.26, considering weight reductions of 50% and 75%. Our investigation focuses on how varying heat convection coefficients affect the optimized structures. For this study, we set the ambient temperature to 0°C. The optimization process is conducted across a range of heat convection coefficients to thoroughly analyze their influence on the resulting designs. This approach allows us to observe how the optimal topology evolves as convective heat transfer becomes more prominent. It's worth noting that many experts in topology optimization advocate for the use of dimensionless studies. This recommendation stems from the advantages of dimensionless analysis in providing more generalizable results and facilitating easier comparison across different scales and scenarios [76, 77]. In topology optimization research, many scholars employ simplified material constants, often using unity values, to streamline studies and facilitate the development of objective functions and gradient optimizers. This approach, valuable for assessing adjoint analysis techniques, aligns with the broader use of dimensional analysis in engineering and scientific research. Dimensional analysis offers benefits such as problem simplification, identification of key parameters, development of scaling relationships, and experimental design for hypothesis testing. In our dimensionless study, we initially set a low convection rate, given that conduction and thermal expansion coefficients were normalized to 1 for the base material across all design cases. This approach allows for the isolation and examination of convection effects on the optimized structure, as even minor changes in surface boundary convection can significantly impact topology optimization outcomes [78–81].

The findings summarized in Table 5.5 indicate that the design domain adapts to increasing heat convection by developing extensions from the primary design body. Expanding the investigation to include multi‐scale optimization yielded the results presented in Table 5.6. Similar to the single‐scale optimization outcomes, the macroscale design in the multi‐scale case responds to elevated heat convection by forming additional extensions to accommodate the increased thermal load. These results highlight the influence of heat convection on the structural resilience of the design. Notably, as heat convection intensifies, spike‐like formations emerge along the design boundaries, protruding from the main structure. These features become more pronounced with higher convection rates. The elevated convection‐driven heat transfer at the macroscale compels the structure to introduce perpendicular links, which in turn prompts the microstructure to adapt its configuration.

As shown in Table 5.6, the microstructure responds by increasing its shear modulus of elasticity while reducing Young's modulus in the x and y directions. This adaptation enhances the design's resilience and elasticity against deformations induced by thermal actuation while also strengthening its resistance to twisting that could impair linear movements in the x direction—a key objective of the optimization. Additionally, the solid heat conductivity tensor indicates that the microstructure seeks to minimize heat transfer to the newly formed extensions, thereby preserving the thermal efficiency of the main design body.

5.3.3.3.2 Topology optimization for heat‐activated grippers

This section addresses the optimization of a heat‐activated gripper. As shown in Figure 5.23, a heat flow is established from the left boundary to the right, while the upper and lower boundaries of the initial design domain are thermally insulated. Adopting a methodology similar to the numerical example discussed in Section 5.3.4.4.1, and excluding convection effects, we performed a multiphysics topology optimization of the heat‐activated gripper. The analysis is conducted on a single‐scale macro design domain, utilizing the same bilinear finite element discretization as in the prior study for both elastic and heat transfer physics.

The design domain consists of 80 × 80 elements, with a non‐design region for the gripper measuring 40 × 10 elements, forming an opening. Two volume fraction cases, 0.25 and 0.5 (corresponding to 75% and 50% weight reductions, respectively), were analyzed. The gripping output points were positioned above and below the opening, offset by 26 elements (as depicted in Figure 5.32), to provide greater flexibility for topology optimization in achieving an optimal design.

Initially, a single‐scale optimization was performed, focusing exclusively on the macroscale. The optimization was then repeated for the two volume fraction cases. The first case employed a volume fraction of 0.5, achieving a 50% weight reduction, while the second case utilized a volume fraction of 0.25, resulting in a 75% weight reduction. The results of the single‐scale macroscale topology optimization, along with the evolution of the objective functions over time, are presented in Figure 5.33.

We extended the scope of this investigation to encompass the design of a heat‐activated porous gripper. Following a similar methodology to our previous approach, we initialized the microstructure design domain with a circular central sub‐design region of a 50‐element radius, as illustrated in Figure 5.34. The primary goal was to perform multi‐scale optimization targeting a 50% weight reduction at both macro‐ and microscales while ensuring an overall volume fraction of 0.25, corresponding to a total weight reduction of 75%. This approach assumed that each element's volume was equivalent to that of the microscale. By simultaneously minimizing weights at both scales, a significant volume reduction was achieved. The optimization results for both scales are depicted in Figure 5.35.

The single‐scale optimization design that aimed at a 50% weight reduction exhibited inferior performance compared to the design that optimized for a 75% weight reduction, as shown in Figure 5.36. However, it is important to note that both single‐ and multi‐scale structures that optimized for a 75% weight reduction displayed comparable performance.

To further investigate the influence of convection on gripper design, we analyzed both the 75% and 50% weight reduction cases, with results presented in Tables 5.7 and 5.8, respectively. Remarkably, the multi‐scale porous gripper that optimized for a 75% weight reduction demonstrated excellent performance, comparable to that of the single‐scale design achieving the same weight reduction.

During single‐scale optimization, it has been noted that as heat transfer to the surroundings increases (reflected by a higher heat convection coefficient), the gripper design demonstrates greater flexibility. However, while the mechanical contact between the point of action and the mechanical support is maintained, the design progressively becomes less effective for its intended function. This phenomenon is more pronounced in cases of 75% weight reduction compared to 50% weight reduction, particularly when heat convection is relatively low. This behavior is illustrated in Tables 5.7 and 5.8. The larger surface area associated with the 50% weight reduction serves as a thermal energy reservoir, contributing to this effect. A similar trend is observed in the multi‐scale optimized cases, as shown in Table 5.9. These results highlight the influence of heat convection on the gripper's performance, where increased heat dissipation to the surroundings diminishes its functionality. The thermal energy reservoir created by the larger surface area further amplifies this impact. These findings emphasize the importance of carefully managing and balancing heat convection effects during the optimization process to ensure the gripper's practicality and performance.

To examine the dependence of the macrostructure on microstructural systems, multiple microstructures were introduced for concurrent optimization alongside the macrostructure. This analysis was conducted on a heat‐activated gripper by dividing its macro design domain into distinct sections, with each section assigned a unique micro design domain. While the size, initial design variables, and spatial configurations remained consistent, each micro design domain was assigned an individual finite element system. Initially, the macro domain was divided into four equal sections, each with its own micro design domain, as depicted in Figure 5.37. The multi‐scale design maintained a volume fraction of 0.25, corresponding to a 75% weight reduction, and the resulting design is shown in Figure 5.38. Additionally, the macro domain was further divided into eight sections, each with a designated micro design domain, as illustrated in Figure 5.39, with the final design presented in Figure 5.40.

As illustrated in Figure 5.41, the design's performance markedly increased with the addition of more sections and a tailored microstructure. This enhancement was especially notable in the eight‐section case, where performance improved by an impressive 31.3% compared to the single macroscale optimized design with a 50% weight reduction (refer to case [a] in Figure 5.33). These results underscore that enhancing the microstructural diversity within the macroscale domain allows concurrently optimized multi‐scale designs to achieve remarkable adaptability.

The material properties of the periodic microstructure were evaluated using asymptotic homogenization theory. However, the core assumptions of homogenization theory—scale separation and periodicity—do not inherently address the connectivity between adjacent microstructures during the optimization of multi‐microstructure systems. Insufficient microstructural connections, particularly at the boundaries of adjoining sections, are a primary source of connectivity issues, compounded by challenges in manufacturability (as illustrated in Figures 5.38(b) and 5.40(b)). Moreover, weak connectivity can lead to difficulties in load transfer and inaccuracies in property estimation when applying the homogenization approach. Therefore, it is crucial to examine the influence of boundary conditions and connectivity between microstructures to achieve practical and manufacturable designs suitable for additive manufacturing. This issue has been explored by several researchers. For example, Du et al. [82] investigated the introduction of a connectivity index to control the connection of the adjacent microstructures. Liu et al. [83] investigated the introduction of predefined connectivity regions for microstructures. In this research, we adopted Neves et al.'s [84] method by implementing a connection boundary as a non‐design domain to ensure better connectivity on the microstructure level. Also, this method showed attaining very good results in terms of solving the connectivity issues of microstructure and, therefore, improving the manufacturing ability [85, 86]. The optimization process is repeated using a connective boundary with a width of four elements for both the four‐section and eight‐section cases, as depicted in Figure 5.42(a) and (b), respectively. For the multi‐scale cases, a volume fraction of 0.25 is maintained, corresponding to a 75% weight reduction. The resulting optimization outcomes are presented in Figures 5.43 and 5.44.

The effectiveness of concurrently optimized multi‐scale designs improves markedly as the heterogeneity of the microstructure within the macroscale domain increases [3, 20]. Nevertheless, in multi‐scale and multi‐sector design domains that include connected non‐design regions, their performance is significantly reduced compared to scenarios without connectivity constraints, as illustrated in Figure 5.45. This reduction arises from the restrictions on microstructural movements imposed by the fixed framework. These limitations curtail the adaptability of the microstructure and obstruct the fine‐tuning of design parameters, ultimately leading to diminished performance. These results highlight the critical need to address connectivity constraints to achieve optimal outcomes in multi‐scale design domains.

Chapter 6
Advanced Software Utilization for Designing and Analyzing Porous Structures

6.1 INTRODUCTION

In this chapter, we provide an overview of commercial tools commonly used for designing porous structures and introduce educational codes to illustrate the principles of porous structure design. The chapter is divided into two main sections. The first section presents several widely used software platforms that support the modeling and optimization of porous architectures for industrial applications. These tools were selected based on practical experience and technical familiarity, rather than as endorsements or commercial promotions. The goal is to provide insights into the features and capabilities of these software platforms, enhancing the understanding of current design methodologies and their application in additive manufacturing. The second section offers demonstrations of educational codes for designing porous structures through topology optimization. These examples are intended for educational purposes only and are designed to provide foundational knowledge of the underlying concepts. It is important to clarify that, while every effort has been made to ensure the accuracy and reliability of the educational codes provided, the author does not guarantee that the codes are free of errors or bugs. The author shall not be held liable for any claims, damages, or issues arising from the use of these codes, whether during or after their execution. Users are encouraged to use these codes responsibly and at their own risk.

6.2 COMMERCIAL SOFTWARE

In this section, we review several commercial software packages commonly employed for designing porous structures. The current approach to designing porous structures for the vast majority of commercial software predominantly relies on infilling the geometry with predesigned microstructures, with the mechanical behavior often approximated using empirical interpolation functions based on the microstructure's size and thickness. While this method provides a straightforward solution, it oversimplifies the complex interactions within the material, limiting the precision of the mechanical response prediction. Software vendors have recognized the need to improve reliability by minimizing the risks associated with generating microstructures through optimization techniques, such as inverse homogenization. However, despite these advancements, inverse homogenization has yet to be fully integrated as a direct design tool into most commercial software platforms. This omission significantly restricts the potential for more innovative, performance‐driven designs. Although some software offers the flexibility to develop custom microstructures via computer language interfaces, enabling users to write code, this remains a workaround rather than a fully realized solution.

6.2.1 Altair Inspire

Altair Inspire is a simulation software that has become a valuable tool for creating and optimizing lightweight structures, particularly through lattice optimization techniques. By combining various lattice geometries, it enables the creation of designs that are lighter, stronger, and more energy‐efficient by adapting porous structures. In Altair Inspire, lattice optimization transforms the elements of a three‐dimensional (3D) mesh of solid structure components into interconnected lattice beams. The size and thickness of these beams are determined using a gray area approximation within the solid isotropic material with penalization (SIMP) [1–3] method. By interpolating the design variables of each element and its surrounding elements, the thickness of the lattice beams is defined.

However, the lattice design itself is preconfigured, with lattice types selected from a library of predefined options embedded in the software. Therefore, it is more accurate to describe this process as a hybrid non‐parametric optimization design approach.

6.2.2 GENESIS Software

In some similarity with Altair Inspire, using the GENESIS software can optimize lattice structures by employing topometry optimization, a method that enables the adjustment of lattice member dimensions based on specific design criteria. GENESIS streamlines this process through its use of the homogenization method. The adaptation of asymptotic homogenization allows for fast simulation without the need to explicitly model the individual lattice elements. By selecting key parameters such as lattice cell type, unit cell dimensions, and cross‐sectional thickness, users can efficiently define the structural characteristics of the lattice within finite element analysis simulations. The software offers a wide library of prebuilt lattice configurations, including cubic, body‐centered cubic, and double pyramid geometries, providing flexibility in selecting the lattice pattern. The optimization process involves minimizing some properties and functions such as mass while constraining displacements.

6.2.3 nTopology

nTopology is a non‐parametric optimization‐based design software that offers an approach to creating porous structures, particularly tailored to additive manufacturing applications. With its advanced computational design tools, nTopology allows to select lattice structures that are controlled to introduce porosity for the structure, to allow light weighting while optimizing structural performance as stiffness.

The software supports a wide range of lattice configurations including triply periodic minimal surfaces (TPMS), such as gyroid and Diamond. TPMS structures are particularly useful in optimizing material efficiency and mechanical performance, as they exhibit continuous, smooth surfaces that can distribute stresses evenly and improve structural integrity without adding excess weight.

Another key aspect of nTopology is its implicit modeling approach. With implicit modeling, geometric operations are defined using mathematical functions, making it easier to create complex shapes like lattices that would otherwise be labor‐intensive or even impossible to model explicitly.

6.2.4 SpaceClaim

Ansys SpaceClaim is a versatile 3D modeling software developed to design porous structures. Once the desired unit cell type is selected, SpaceClaim allows these cells to be populated within a predefined design domain. The software provides some flexibility to control parameters such as cell size, orientation, and distribution, enabling optimization of the properties of the porous material.

6.2.5 PTC Creo

PTC Creo is a computer‐aided design software that provides a set of tools for the design and analysis of products for mechanical engineering. One of the features of Creo is its lattice design functionality, which supports the creation of various types of porous structures. These include beam‐based lattices, 2.5D lattices [4, 5], formula‐driven lattices [6–11], custom lattices, and stochastic lattices [12–14], each offering unique advantages for different engineering applications. Creo's lattice generation tools allow us to optimize structures for strength, weight, thermal conductivity, and manufacturability.

6.3 CODING‐BASED COMMERCIAL SOFTWARE DESIGN OF POROUS STRUCTURES

Several software packages allow users to customize features by utilizing programming languages or even introducing new functionalities through their own programming platforms, such as APD for ANSYS and ANSA coding for Beta software. Additionally, widely used high‐level programming languages like Java, VBA, TCL, MATLAB, and Python are supported by these software packages. These languages, with their extensive libraries and large community support, enable the rapid development of cost‐effective, high‐performance custom programs. Such programs can enhance the design of porous structures and even facilitate the creation of porous structures in software that does not natively support such designs. For example, the author developed a topology optimization‐based porous structure design using MATLAB to control COMSOL software, enabling the design of elastic failure‐resistant microstructures for various theories of elastic and dynamic failures, as depicted in Figure 6.1.

Moreover, it is possible to program commercial software that does not natively support programming languages. For example, Nastran has been shown to be adaptable for use with C++, enabling the development of innovative porous designs, such as porous laminated plates, to withstand static and dynamic loads (as depicted in Figure 6.2) [15].

6.3.1 Software Multiphysics Topology Optimization

The concurrent multi‐scale optimization steps for the multiphysics problem outlined in Section 5‐2‐8 are implemented in a MATLAB code, which is provided in the GitHub respiratory (https://github.com/musaddiqalali/Multiscal-multiphysic-topology-optimization). This code employs the SIMP method for topology optimization to effectively solve the problem. The MATLAB code for topology optimization and homogenization, based on the work by Andreassen et al. [16, 17], serves as the foundation for this implementation. The code is structured into seven distinct modules, each organized into separate MATLAB files to provide flexibility and customization for the user. These modules include:

  1. Input Data: Handles the initial parameters and setup;
  2. Finite Element Preparations: Prepares the finite element method (FEM) framework;
  3. Normalization Factor Calculation: Computes necessary normalization factors;
  4. Asymptotic Homogenization: Applies homogenization techniques to model material properties;
  5. Goal Programming Multi‐Objective Function: Constructs the multi‐objective optimization function;
  6. ConcurrentMulti‐scale Optimization: Executes the optimization process across multiple scales.

These modules are distributed across seven MATLAB functions:

  • Concurrent_MOO.m,
  • get_Initials.m,
  • Concurrent_solver.m,
  • Homogenization_full.m,
  • elementMatVec.m,
  • Objective_Calculator.m,
  • OC_2D.m.

The main program, Concurrent_MOO.m, orchestrates the entire optimization process, including data generation and execution of the core optimization steps. The other functions are called within this main program to perform specific tasks as part of the overall process. The flow of the program is illustrated in Figure 6.3, which provides a visual representation of how the functions interact.

The main program is contained in the primary MATLAB file, (Concurrent_MOO.m), and begins at line 4, as illustrated in Figure 6.4. At this point, the microscale input parameters are defined, including (Micro_nelx) and (Micro_nely), which represent the number of elements in the x and y directions, respectively.

The microscale volume fraction constraint vm is defined by (Micro_volfrac), while (penal) represents the power of the SIMP power‐law function. The (Micro_rmin) parameter specifies the radius for the mesh independence filter, which will be further explained in the Optimality Criteria section. Similarly, the macroscale initial design domain and spatial configurations are defined starting at line 6. The macroscale input parameters include (Macro_nelx) and (Macro_nely), which denote the number of elements in the x and y directions, respectively. The volume fraction constraint for the macroscale is specified by (Macro_volfrac), which is defined at line 7. Additionally, the element aspect ratios for the macroscale spatial configuration are represented by (dx) and (dy).

In line 9, the maximum number of iterations for the optimization process, (maxIter), is specified. In our analysis, two models (i.e., the heat and the mechanical physics) are constructed to calculate the heat and mechanical compliances. As a result, two separate finite element systems are employed. Since the SIMP method is based on an Eulerian framework, where the element weight, represented by the design variable x, is gradually reduced rather than eliminating undesired elements during the topology optimization process, the finite element framework can be prepared in advance of the optimization. Both the heat and mechanical analyses utilize a structured bilinear element with a uniform length of unity, as illustrated in Figure 6.5.

For the mechanical compliance analysis, the finite element preparation steps are outlined in lines 11–22 (as shown in Figure 6.6). The initial degree of freedom (DOF) index for each element is determined using the matrix (Macro_nodenrs_mech), which is stored in a vector format called (Macro_edofVec_mech). This vector is then used to calculate the indices of the eight DOF for each element, which are stored in the DOF matrix (Macro_edofMat_mech). This matrix facilitates the efficient construction of the stiffness matrix during the optimization loop. For the example shown in Figure 6.7, which consists of 25 elements, the matrix (Macro_edofMat_mech) is

The (Macro_edofMat_mech) matrix is then used to generate the row and column index vectors, (iK_mech) and (jK_mech), respectively, as shown in lines 15 and 16. A Kronecker product is performed with an 8‐length unit vector, followed by a reshaping procedure. These two index vectors are used to construct the global mechanical stiffness matrix for the macroscale case. Since the Eulerian approach is employed, the global stiffness matrix remains unchanged throughout the entire optimization process, as previously mentioned.

The displacement and force values are stored in the computer's RAM in lines 17 and 18. The boundary conditions for the studied cases are defined in lines 19 and 20. The free DOF are determined in line 22 by subtracting the fixed DOF (identified in line 20) from the total DOF (specified in line 21). As shown in Figure 6.7, the DOF for the macroscale in the x and y directions are represented by red and blue colors, respectively. The micro design domain follows a similar approach for assigning DOF.

Similarly, for the heat conductivity problem, the finite element preparations are in a similar order of mechanical stiffness case, and they are listed in lines 24–34 (as shown in Figure 6.8).

The next step is to calculate the factors that are used for the goal programming, i.e., utopia points () and nadir points (). These points are calculated for each objective function corresponding to each physics. The calculations are performed using dedicated MATLAB functions: (Concurrent_solver.m) and (Objective_Calculator.m). The nadir points, representing the mechanical and thermal compliances of the initial design, are also determined during this process (i.e., and , respectively), as well as the mechanical utopia point () and the heat utopia point (), which are needed to perform the multi‐objective optimization (which are the separately optimized results for each physical model); therefore, in lines 36–46, the values of and are calculated (as shown in Figure 6.9). Also, and are calculated in lines 49–58, as shown in Figure 6.10. As shown in Figures 6.9 and 6.10, this process necessitates the call of several MATLAB functions.

In line 36, the initial sets for both the macro‐ and microscales are calculated using the sub‐function (get_Initials) (found in the file (get_Initials.m)). This function constructs the initial macroscale set, (Macro_x), and the initial microscale set, (Micro_x). The macroscale design domain is initialized with a value of 0.5, as shown in Figure 6.11. In line 38, the design variables (Macro_x) and (Micro_x) are assigned to intermediate variables (Macro_xPhys) and (Micro_xPhys), respectively. This step is performed to prevent any accumulation of errors during further calculations.

In the MATLAB implementation of homogenization (i.e., (Homogenization_full.m)), the finite element used is also bilinear, as shown in Figure 6.12. The RVE in this case consists of a composite of two distinct phases: material and void. To streamline the computation and reduce processing time, Lamé's constants [19] are employed in the representation. This approach allows the calculation of both thermal and mechanical effective properties within a single homogenization code. The same homogenization program is used to calculate both the thermal and mechanical properties of the designed representative volume element (RVE). To distinguish between the two cases, the identifier flag (select_case) is used. For evaluating the elastic tensor, (select_case) is set to 1, while for calculating the heat conductivity tensor, it is set to 2. Lines 2–11 are dedicated to preparing the input data for the homogenization process.

In line 8, Lambda is a vector that contains Lame's first parameter (λ) for the material and void. Similarly, mu is a vector with Lamé's second parameter (μ). Furthermore, in finite element systems, both the problems and the applied loads are treated as dimensionless. The design domain is assumed to be rectangular, and finite element analyses for both the micro‐ and macro systems are conducted using square elements with a uniform side length of 1. The mesh is structured uniformly across the entire design domain to ensure consistency and computational efficiency. Each physical model shares the same structural element and the design variables xM and xm ((Macro_x) and (Micro_x), respectively)). An example of a finite element mesh consisting of 25 elements of nodal temperature (T), nodal displacement in x and y directions (U and V, respectively), and element density fields of xM and xm is presented in Figure 6.13.

The optimization of the heat conduction physical model is performed using the volume‐to‐point approach, which necessitates a uniformly distributed heat load to be applied across the entire macroscale design domain. The heat load is set to be 0.01 (P = 0.01 in line 30). The boundaries are adiabatic except for the section of zero temperature portion to represent the place of the heat sink (line 31). In general, heat compliance problem is by nature a monotonously decreasing function [21], so volume fraction is important to implement a robust design, and for our design problems, volume fraction is set to be 0.5 weight reduction as a condition to be satisfied with the optimization process for both the macro (vM)‐ and microscale (vm) (Macro_volfrac = Micro_volfrac = 0.5). The heat compliance and the sensitivity analysis are calculated, and the sensitivity function is hierarchically inferring the macro design variables. The microstructure has been solved with periodic boundary conditions. Heat compliance sensitivity in terms of the micro design domain is used to update the values of the micro design domain variables simultaneously by updating the macro design set at each iteration. For the physical model of mechanical compliance, the design domain is fixed from the support points such that the displacement in the x and y directions is set to zero. The force of magnitude 1 is assigned to the loading points. The modeling and sensitivity of the multi‐scale case are similar to what has been discussed in the heat transfer case.

6.3.2 Graphical User Interface‐Based Multi‐scale Optimization Tools for Porous Heat Sink

In this section, and to facilitate practical applications, we have provided three MATLAB‐based executable files, namely Muti_TopOptC.exe for the initial circular hole microstructure, Muti_TopOptS.exe for the initial square hole microstructure, and Muti_TopOptR.exe for the initial rhombus hole microstructure. These tools are accessible in the GitHub repository at https://github.com/musaddiqalali/Numerical-tools-.git, enabling the design of both macro‐ and microscales for the aforementioned problem and generating the resulting designs in JPEG format within the run directory. Here, we provide a detailed explanation of an example illustrating the concurrent topology optimization tool, focusing on the microstructure with a central circular sub‐design domain. It's important to note that the steps involved in this example are analogous to those followed in the other tools described earlier in this document. This particular case will serve as a representative demonstration of how to effectively employ the concurrent topology optimization methodology, offering insights into the process and demonstrating its applicability in a practical context.

The executable program (Muti_TopOptC.exe) features a user‐friendly graphic interface (GUI), which is prominently depicted in Figure 6.16. This GUI serves as an intuitive and visually accessible platform for interacting with the software.

The inputs from top to bottom are penal which refers to the penalization (p). The second input is volfrac which refers to the volume fraction of the macroscale. My and Mx are the number of elements in the x and y directions, respectively, also for the macroscale. Here, each element has the dimension of 1 mm × 1 mm. Finally, rmin is assigned using rmin input. For the microscale system, the same macroscale penalization is used. The volume fraction of the microstructure is assigned by m_volfrac. The side size of the square microscale design domain is set using the variable Micro Size. The micro design domain's initial design has a central circular hole of around 20% of the total area of the square micro design domain. So, for the microstructure of 100 × 100 element discretization, it has a circular sub‐design domain of the radius of 50 elements. After entering all the necessary information, the user may press the Optimize button to begin the optimization process. A progress bar will appear on the screen (as presented in Figure 6.17).

This also means that the optimization is in progress. Also, the Readiness Indicator is green which means the data are all inserted properly. If there are missing data, the Readiness Indicator becomes white. When pressing the Optimize button in this case, a message will appear indicating that the missing data are still not entered (as shown in Figure 6.18).

The output of the program is two figures which present the macro‐ and microscales of the multi‐scale problem. An example of the naming is presented in Figure 6.19.

We provided another two MATLAB‐based executable files of the name (Muti_TopOptS.exe for the initial square hole on the microstructure and Muti_TopOptR.exe for the microstructure of the initial rhombus hole) in the GitHub respiratory (https://github.com/musaddiqalali/Numerical-tools-.git) to design the macro‐ and microscales for the above problem and produce the design as a JPEG image within the run directory. It is important to indicate that these files necessitate the preinstallation of MATLAB runtime 9.7 or higher, which can be downloaded freely from (https://www.mathworks.com/products/compiler/matlab-runtime.html). After installing the MATLAB runtime 9.7, the programs can be run without a problem.

Chapter 7
Emerging Trends and Directions in Advanced Porous Structures

7.1 INTRODUCTION

Porous materials, defined by their interconnected voids and high surface area, have become essential in numerous advanced applications, including filtration, catalysis, energy storage, and biomedical devices. The growing demand for materials with customized properties has spurred the development of nanotechnology and microfabrication techniques that enable precise control over pore structures. These advancements, fueled by nanotechnology and microfabrication, allow for the creation of highly functional porous materials with superior performance compared to traditional methods. This article explores key nanotechnology and microfabrication techniques for creating advanced porous structures and highlights their applications and associated challenges.

Porous materials are categorized based on their pore size: macropores (>50 nm), mesopores (2–50 nm), and micropores (<2 nm). Each category offers distinct advantages for specific applications. For example, in catalysis, pore geometry influences the diffusion of reactants and products, while in biomedical contexts, pore size and distribution are critical for cellular growth and tissue regeneration. Traditional methods like sintering, templating, and foaming, while effective, often lack the precision required for nanoscale control. Emerging nanotechnology and microfabrication techniques, particularly lithography‐based approaches, have transformed the ability to create porous materials with controlled porosity and intricate geometries.

7.2 ADVANCED MANUFACTURING

7.2.1 Lithography‐Based Techniques

Lithography, initially developed for semiconductor manufacturing, has become a cornerstone for fabricating nano‐ and microscale porous structures. Variants such as ultraviolet (UV) lithography, electron‐beam lithography, and nanoimprint lithography (NIL) provide unparalleled precision and customization.

7.2.1.1 UV Lithography

This technique uses UV light to pattern photosensitive materials, achieving sub‐micron resolutions. UV lithography is widely used for fabricating ordered porous structures in microelectronics, photonics, and sensors. However, its resolution is limited by the wavelength of UV light, restricting the creation of finer features [1, 2].

7.2.1.2 Electron‐Beam Lithography (e‐beam)

Offering nanometer‐scale resolution, e‐beam lithography uses a focused electron beam to write patterns directly onto a resist material. This method is ideal for creating nanoporous structures with intricate designs, such as nanopore arrays and mesoporous films. However, its slow writing speed limits scalability for large‐scale production [3–5].

7.2.1.3 Nanoimprint Lithography

NIL employs a mold to imprint patterns onto a resist material, achieving resolutions below 10 nm. It is a high‐throughput technique suitable for producing large‐area porous structures used in sensors, energy devices, and drug delivery systems [6–8].

7.2.2 Etching and Deposition Techniques

Etching and deposition processes complement lithography by enabling the selective removal or addition of materials to control pore geometry.

7.2.2.1 Dry Etching

Techniques like reactive ion etching (RIE) and deep reactive ion etching (DRIE) use ionized gases to remove materials, creating well‐defined, high‐aspect‐ratio pores. DRIE, in particular, is instrumental in fabricating deep microstructures for microfluidics, energy harvesting, and biomedical devices [9–11].

7.3 APPLICATIONS OF ADVANCED POROUS STRUCTURES

The precise control over pore properties achieved through nanotechnology and microfabrication techniques has led to widespread applications across various fields:

7.3.1 Energy Storage and Conversion

The global demand for sustainable and efficient energy storage systems has driven significant research into novel materials that can enhance the performance of energy storage and conversion devices. Porous materials have emerged as a promising class of materials for energy storage and conversion applications due to their high surface area, tunable porosity, and unique properties that allow for the efficient storage, transfer, and conversion of energy. By precisely controlling the pore shape and size, it is possible to design materials that optimize the performance of batteries and supercapacitors. This article explores the role of porous materials in energy storage and conversion, with a focus on how controlling pore structures can enhance their efficiency and functionality.

Porous materials are characterized by the presence of voids or pores within their structure. These materials can be classified based on the size of their pores into microporous (pore size <2 nm), mesoporous (2–50 nm), and macroporous (>50 nm) materials. The high surface area associated with porous materials makes them ideal candidates for applications that require efficient energy storage and conversion, as the surface area plays a critical role in processes such as charge storage, ion transport, and catalysis. The ability to control the size, shape, and distribution of pores in these materials opens up opportunities for tailoring their properties to specific energy‐related applications. For example, in batteries and supercapacitors, controlling the pore structure can influence the rate of ion diffusion and the total charge storage capacity. In fuel cells and catalytic processes, the pore structure can enhance the surface area available for chemical reactions, improving the efficiency of energy conversion. In the following section, we will delve into energy storage devices such as batteries and supercapacitors, exploring how advanced porous materials contribute to their performance.

7.3.1.1 Batteries

Batteries store energy through electrochemical reactions, where ions are stored in the electrode materials and released during discharge. The performance of a battery is influenced by the rate of ion diffusion, the surface area of the electrodes, and the material's ability to accommodate ions during charge and discharge cycles. Porous materials, especially those with well‐controlled pore sizes, have shown promise in enhancing the performance of batteries, particularly lithium‐ion and sodium‐ion batteries [12–14].

For batteries, the size of the pores in the electrode material influences the rate at which ions can diffuse through the material. Smaller pores can facilitate faster ion diffusion, which is critical for improving the charge/discharge rates of batteries. However, excessively small pores can lead to slower ion transport due to increased resistance. Therefore, it is essential to balance the pore size to achieve optimal ion diffusion and energy storage capacity. As an application, porous carbon‐based materials, such as activated carbon, graphene, and carbon nanotubes, have been widely used in battery electrodes due to their high surface area and conductivity [15–18].

By controlling the pore size and distribution, researchers can optimize the structure of these materials to enhance ion storage and improve battery performance. For example, mesoporous carbons with a well‐defined pore structure can increase the surface area for ion storage while allowing for fast ion diffusion, leading to higher capacity and faster charging times. Moreover, nanostructured electrodes, such as those made from porous silicon or metal oxides, have also shown great promise in improving battery performance. Porous silicon, for example, can expand and contract during cycling without significant structural degradation, making it a suitable candidate for high‐capacity anodes. By controlling the pore structure, it is possible to mitigate issues related to volume expansion and improve the cycling stability of these materials [19–21].

7.3.1.2 Supercapacitors

Supercapacitors store energy through electrostatic charge accumulation on the surface of the electrodes, making them ideal for applications that require rapid charge and discharge cycles. The performance of supercapacitors is primarily determined by the surface area and pore structure of the electrode materials. A high surface area and well‐defined pore sizes enable the efficient accumulation of charge, leading to higher capacitance and energy density. Microporous carbon‐based materials, with pore sizes less than 2 nm, are commonly used in supercapacitor electrodes due to their ability to store charge at the molecular level [22, 23]. By controlling the pore size and distribution, researchers can optimize the material for higher energy and power densities. For example, a well‐controlled microporous structure can provide a high surface area for charge accumulation while maintaining a high rate of ion diffusion, resulting in fast charge/discharge cycles. On the other hand, mesoporous carbon and metal oxide materials are also used in supercapacitors, offering a balance between high surface area and ion diffusion rates. Mesoporous materials provide additional space for charge storage while allowing for faster ion transport, leading to improved power density. The combination of microporous and mesoporous structures in hybrid materials has been shown to further enhance the performance of supercapacitors by optimizing both energy and power densities [24–26].

7.3.2 Biomedical Applications

Porous structures have become integral to advancements in biomedical engineering due to their unique ability to replicate the natural microenvironments of biological tissues. These materials are increasingly employed in tissue engineering, drug delivery systems, and implantable devices, leveraging their tunable pore architectures to address complex medical challenges. One of the most prominent applications of porous structures in biomedicine is in tissue engineering. Porous scaffolds act as artificial extracellular matrices (ECMs), providing a three‐dimensional framework that supports cell attachment, proliferation, and differentiation. The design of these scaffolds involves tailoring pore size, shape, and interconnectivity to mimic the physical and biochemical cues of natural tissues. The ability of porous scaffolds to facilitate cell growth is heavily influenced by pore size and geometry. For instance, scaffolds with pore sizes in the range of 100–150 μm are ideal for osteoblast proliferation and bone tissue regeneration, while smaller pore sizes (<50 μm) are suitable for chondrocyte growth in cartilage repair. Interconnected pores ensure efficient nutrient and oxygen transport, which is critical for maintaining cell viability in large constructs. Biodegradable polymers such as polylactic acid (PLA), polyglycolic acid (PGA), and their copolymers are commonly used for scaffold fabrication. These materials degrade over time, allowing the newly formed tissue to replace the scaffold. Additionally, bioceramics like hydroxyapatite and tricalcium phosphate are incorporated into porous scaffolds to enhance their mechanical properties and bioactivity, particularly in bone tissue engineering [27–31].

The ability to engineer pore size and distribution allows for precise modulation of drug release kinetics, enhancing therapeutic efficacy while minimizing side effects. Porous structures enable multiple mechanisms of drug release, including diffusion, degradation, and swelling. In diffusion‐controlled systems, drugs are encapsulated within the pores, and their release rate is governed by the pore size and interconnectivity. In degradation‐controlled systems, the material matrix gradually breaks down, releasing the encapsulated drug over time. Swelling‐controlled systems, often made of hydrogels, release drugs as the material absorbs water and expands [32–35]. Porous nanoparticles, such as mesoporous silica nanoparticles (MSNs), have emerged as promising drug carriers for cancer therapy. These nanoparticles can be functionalized with targeting ligands to deliver chemotherapeutic agents directly to tumor cells, reducing systemic toxicity. The high surface area of MSNs allows for a high drug‐loading capacity, while their tunable pore sizes enable controlled release profiles. Advanced porous materials are being developed to respond to specific physiological stimuli, such as pH, temperature, or enzymatic activity. For instance, pH‐sensitive porous hydrogels release drugs in acidic environments, such as tumor microenvironments, ensuring site‐specific delivery. Similarly, thermo‐responsive porous polymers release drugs upon reaching a certain temperature, which can be achieved through external heating or localized inflammation. Porous structures are integral to the design of implantable devices, particularly in orthopedic and dental applications [36–39]. Their porosity enhances osseointegration and reduces stress shielding, improving the long‐term performance of implants. Porous titanium and its alloys are widely used in bone implants due to their excellent biocompatibility and mechanical properties. The interconnected porosity of these implants promotes vascularization and new bone formation, ensuring stable fixation. Additive manufacturing techniques have enabled the fabrication of titanium implants with complex porous architectures, closely mimicking the natural bone. Porous materials are also employed in soft tissue implants, such as breast prostheses and dermal fillers. In these applications, the porosity of the material affects its integration with surrounding tissues and its ability to retain shape over time [40–43]. One major challenge is achieving the optimal balance between porosity and mechanical strength. Highly porous materials may lack the mechanical stability required for load‐bearing applications, necessitating innovative design strategies [44–49]. Advances in computational modeling are enabling the design of multi‐scale porous structures that optimize mechanical and biological performance. These models integrate microscale features, such as pore geometry, with macroscale properties, such as overall stiffness, to create hierarchical structures tailored to specific applications.

7.3.3 The Role of Porous Materials in Advanced Optical Technologies

Porous materials have become foundational in photonics, leveraging their unique structural, optical, and electrical properties for innovations in light manipulation, sensing, and communication. Their nano‐ and microscale architectures enable precise control over light–matter interactions, making them critical for applications ranging from high‐power laser delivery to quantum cryptography and decryption. In photonic devices, porous materials offer a high surface area, tunable refractive indices, and customizable pore structures, enhancing sensitivity and functionality. Nanoporous materials like MSNs and metal–organic frameworks (MOFs) excel in optical sensors by adsorbing target molecules, altering refractive indices or light absorption properties for high‐precision detection. Porous photonic crystals, with their periodic structures, manipulate light via photonic band gaps, enabling waveguides, optical filters, and light‐emitting devices. For high‐power laser systems, porous materials address challenges like heat dissipation, optical damage, and beam quality. Porous ceramics and aerogels manage thermal loads, while nanoporous waveguides enhance beam shaping and reduce divergence. Additionally, antireflective and damage‐resistant coatings from porous materials improve the durability of laser optics. In quantum photonics, porous materials are pivotal for secure communication and encryption.

Quantum dots in porous matrices [50] serve as efficient single‐photon sources for quantum key distribution (QKD), while porous photonic waveguides facilitate low‐loss photon propagation and active material integration. These structures enable photon manipulation, entanglement, and the creation of quantum circuits for advanced operations. Porous substrates also underpin quantum random number generators (QRNGs), ensuring secure encryption through the inherent randomness of quantum processes. Despite their potential, challenges like fabrication complexity, material stability, and integration with existing systems persist. Advances in nanotechnology and microfabrication techniques, as well as the development of robust materials, are essential for realizing the full potential of porous materials in photonics [51–55].

7.4 CHALLENGES AND FUTURE DIRECTIONS

The advent of nanotechnology and microfabrication techniques has significantly advanced the production of porous materials, offering unprecedented control over pore structure, size, and distribution. These innovations have enabled the development of highly specialized materials for a variety of applications, from biomedical implants to energy storage devices. However, several key challenges remain, which hinder the broader implementation of these materials in practical applications. Scalability, cost, and material limitations are among the primary obstacles that need to be addressed. While laboratory‐scale fabrication of porous structures can achieve high precision and complexity, translating these processes to larger scales remains a formidable challenge. Scaling up often results in a loss of structural integrity, dimensional accuracy, and uniformity, leading to variations in the material's properties. Moreover, the costs associated with advanced fabrication techniques, such as electron beam lithography or focused ion beam milling, are prohibitively high for large‐scale production. The need for expensive equipment and the time‐consuming nature of these processes further exacerbate the issue, making it difficult to achieve the cost‐effective production of porous materials.

In addition to scalability and cost, material limitations also pose significant challenges. While a wide range of materials, including metals, ceramics, and polymers, can be used in the fabrication of porous structures, each material presents its own set of challenges. For instance, metallic porous materials, while offering excellent mechanical strength, often suffer from poor chemical resistance and limited processability. On the other hand, polymers and composites, though more versatile, may lack the required mechanical properties or stability under extreme conditions. As such, the development of new materials that combine desirable properties—such as high mechanical strength, chemical resistance, and processability—remains a key area of research.

Furthermore, the integration of porous materials into practical applications often requires overcoming challenges related to mechanical stability, chemical resistance, and reproducibility. Porous structures are inherently weaker than solid materials, and their mechanical stability can be compromised under load or during environmental exposure. This is particularly problematic in applications where the material must withstand high stresses or harsh chemical environments, such as in aerospace or automotive industries. Additionally, ensuring the reproducibility of porous structures is critical for maintaining consistency in performance across batches. Variations in pore size, distribution, or material composition can significantly affect the properties of the final product, making it difficult to guarantee uniformity and reliability.
